## Page 1


Wormholes as red herrings: reflection positivity and
the reconstruction of unitary quantum field theories
Jacob McNamaraa and Zhencheng Wangb
aSimons Center for Geometry and Physics, Stony Brook University,
Stony Brook, NY 11794, USA
bDepartment of Physics, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign,
Urbana, IL 61801, USA
E-mail: jmcnamara@scgp.stonybrook.edu, zcwang1@illinois.edu
Abstract: As Coleman famously argued, the apparent breakdown of partition-function
factorization in quantum gravity associated with Euclidean wormholes is a red herring,
arising from a hidden average over an ensemble of theories. We present a direct analog of
Coleman’s argument for the apparent breakdown of Hilbert-space factorization associated
with spatial wormholes, i.e., Einstein–Rosen bridges.
Our main result is the following
reconstruction theorem for quantum field theories: unitary QFTs are determined, up to
unitary isomorphism, by their closed-manifold partition functions; every reflection-positive
partition function arises from a unitary quantum field theory; and the states prepared by
manifolds span the space of invariant states under the reconstructed theory’s symmetry
group. Interpreting the result gravitationally, we conclude that any apparent breakdown
of Hilbert-space factorization is a red herring, arising from restricting to an incomplete
spectrum of charged states.
arXiv:2607.01322v1  [hep-th]  1 Jul 2026

## Page 4

Introduction
Quantum gravity, though as yet undefined, is meant to be a quantum-mechanical theory
of dynamical spacetime manifolds. In general, quantum mechanics instructs us to com-
pute probability amplitudes by summing the amplitudes for each possible history. This
perspective has proven extremely fruitful in quantum field theory (QFT), as codified in the
Feynman path integral over quantum fields. By analogy, it seems very natural to attempt
to define amplitudes in quantum gravity by summing over all possible spacetime manifolds
in a gravitational path integral (GPI).
The GPI has come in and out of popularity over the years, with a recent surge of
interest following the solution of Jackiw-Teitelboim (JT) gravity in terms of an explicit
evaluation of the GPI [1].
We have learned that the GPI is smarter than previously
thought: not only can it calculate the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy of black holes [2]; it
can even reproduce the Page curve [3] for evaporating black holes once replica wormholes
are included [4–8]. Thus, the GPI seems to give answers entirely consistent with unitary
black hole evaporation, as expected in ultraviolet (UV) complete quantum gravity.
However, despite its successes, the GPI has a severe flaw compared to the familiar path
integral of QFT. In the QFT path integral, the fluctuating quantum fields are local degrees
of freedom living on top of spacetime. These local degrees of freedom may be cut and glued
subject to a division of spacetime into coordinate patches, which makes the locality of the
resulting QFT manifest. Mathematically, this cutting and gluing property is encoded by
the statement that the dynamical fields in the QFT path integral form a sheaf.
In contrast, the fluctuating field in the GPI is a bulk spacetime manifold pinned at
its boundary to some region held fixed as a source. Given a division of the boundary into
pieces, there is no canonical division of the bulk. This is especially true outside of the semi-
classical regime, where we might hope to apply the Ryu–Takayanagi/Hubeny–Rangamani–
Takayanagi [9, 10] or quantum extremal surface [11, 12] prescription, and associate each
boundary region to its entanglement wedge in the bulk. However, even in the semiclassical
regime, the fields in the GPI do not form a sheaf. This is because the entanglement wedge
of a disjoint union may be larger than the disjoint union of the entanglement wedges, con-
taining what is known as an entanglement island [4–8]. The failure of the fields in the
GPI to form a sheaf is a feature, not a bug, as the appearance of entanglement islands is
essential in the recent successes of the GPI discussed above.
Consequently, these recent developments have raised a new tension, known as the
Factorization Paradox, arising from our strong belief in the holographic principle [13–15].
The holographic principle asserts that UV-complete quantum gravity must admit a dual
description in terms of local, non-gravitational degrees of freedom living on the boundary
of spacetime.
There is no tension between holography and the unitarity of black hole
evaporation, and indeed, the two are intimately linked. The tension, instead, is between
the locality of a holographic dual and the nonlocal nature of the GPI. This tension is most
severe when the bulk contains a wormhole connecting two otherwise-disjoint components
of the boundary. Holography tells us that the data associated to a disjoint boundary must
factorize, but a wormhole leads to a connected, non-factorizing contribution to the GPI.
– 2 –


## Page 5


The Factorization Paradox is most commonly viewed as an issue arising from Euclidean
(spacetime) wormholes, meaning wormholes whose Euclidean cross section is codimension-
one in the bulk [16–25]. A Euclidean wormhole describes the propagation of a baby (spa-
tially closed) universe from one boundary component to another. This propagation of baby
universes threatens to spoil the factorization of multi-boundary partition functions,
ζ(M1 ⊔M2) ?= ζ(M1)ζ(M2).
(1.1)
Partition-function factorization is, by now, well understood: the necessary and sufficient
condition for partition-function factorization is that the Hilbert space HBU of baby universe
states is trivial [6, 7, 25–33],
HBU = C,
(1.2)
so that there is no non-trivial propagation of baby universes.
Moreover, we understand what happens when partition-function factorization breaks
down. As classically argued by Coleman [19], and subsequently expanded on by Giddings
and Strominger [20, 21] as well as Marolf and Maxfield [25], this breakdown is always a
red herring. When the GPI produces non-factorizing multi-boundary partition functions
ζ, they may always1 be reinterpreted as the ensemble average of individually-factorizing
partition functions ζα over a measure space of so-called α-sectors,
ζ(M1 ⊔· · · ⊔Mn) =
Z
dα ζα(M1) · · · ζα(Mn).
(1.3)
The original breakdown of partition-function factorization is reinterpreted as the presence
of statistical correlations in the underlying ensemble. One may hope that by projecting to
a fixed α-sector, one could recover the partition function of a single UV-complete theory
of quantum gravity.
However, partition-function factorization is not the end of the story. The factorization
(1.1) of the partition function only ensures that the resulting quantum system is boundary-
local in a very weak sense. In QFT, locality imposes a full tower of constraints, correspond-
ing to the factorization of higher and higher categorical data associated to higher and higher
codimension cuts of spacetime. Correspondingly, the Factorization Paradox consists of a
full tower of possible breakdowns of factorization, which quantify the full tension between
the GPI and the holographic principle.
In this paper, we move one step up this tower, and study the factorization of multi-
boundary Hilbert spaces,
HB1⊔B2
?= HB1 ⊗HB2.
(1.4)
Factorization of Hilbert spaces is certainly threatened by Euclidean wormholes, as the
states on both sides are constrained to lie in the same α-sector. However, projecting onto
a fixed α-sector does not fully resolve Hilbert-space factorization. This is because of the
possibility of spatial wormholes, known as Einstein–Rosen (ER) bridges, which connect
1Up to an unresolved moment problem (as discussed in, e.g., [34–36]).
– 3 –


## Page 6


disjoint spatial slices of the boundary through their bulk codimension-two horizon.
Hilbert-space factorization is less well understood than partition-function factorization.
We expect that, in a UV-complete theory, Hilbert-space factorization is resolved by the ER
= EPR2 proposal [38–40], so that every ER bridge is equivalent to an entangled state in
a tensor-factorized Hilbert space.3 Until now, we have not had a clear characterization of
when ER = EPR must hold, analogous to the necessary and sufficient condition (1.2) for
partition-function factorization. Hilbert-space factorization has been studied in a number
of specific contexts [41–51], with the predominant overarching theme being that Hilbert
spaces seem to factorize once enough degrees of freedom are included.
While we have not known a sufficient condition, we have certainly known a necessary
condition. As first observed by Harlow [41], ER = EPR can never hold if our bulk theory
has a gauge symmetry G with an incomplete spectrum of states [52, 53]. For instance,
if the bulk theory contains a U(1) gauge field without electrically charged matter, then
a Reissner–Nordstr¨om wormhole threaded by electric flux cannot possibly be identified
with an entangled superposition of one-sided states.
This necessary condition forms a
motivational basis for related Swampland constraints including the absence of global 1-
form symmetries [54–58] and the triviality of cobordism classes [59].
1.1
Summary of results
In this paper, we provide a precise characterization of the breakdown of Hilbert-space
factorization, analogous to (1.2), working within the developing axiomatic framework for
the GPI [25, 33, 36, 60–64]. In this axiomatic framework (reviewed in Section 2), we fix a
class X of manifolds with sources, viewed as the boundary conditions of the GPI, as well
as a reflection-positive, factorizing partition function ζ, viewed as the result of projecting
said GPI onto a fixed α-sector.
With X and ζ fixed, we construct a mathematically well-defined invariant, the baby
universe category CBU, such that ER = EPR holds if and only if the baby universe category
is trivial,
CBU = Hilb.
(1.5)
Here, Hilb denotes the category of complex Hilbert spaces and bounded linear maps.4
Heuristically, the objects of CBU are generated, under direct sum and projection onto
subobjects, by the set of closed codimension-two slices of the bulk. The morphisms are
quantum superpositions of bordisms modulo gravitational constraints. Our construction
of CBU is directly motivated and enabled by the previous work [62, 63] of Colafranceschi,
Dong, Marolf, Zhang, and the second author of this paper.
When Hilbert-space factorization breaks down, our definition of CBU allows us to run
a direct analog of Coleman’s argument [19] at one higher category level. The result is the
2“EPR” stands for Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen, who famously discussed quantum entanglement [37].
3Our notion of ER = EPR mainly concerns the direction ER ⇒EPR, and does not require the converse
direction except in some very general, formal sense.
4One might have expected the constraint to be CBU = sHilb, the category of complex super-Hilbert
spaces, or CBU = HilbR, the category of real Hilbert spaces, motivated by [65]. For the definition of CBU
used in most of this paper, (1.5) is the correct constraint. See Section 10.2 for further discussion.
– 4 –


## Page 7


following main theorem, which, while motivated by quantum gravity, is a mathematically
rigorous result in formal QFT alone.
Theorem 1.1 (Main Theorem). Let ζ be a Euclidean partition function defined on a class
X of d-manifolds, possibly with sources, that satisfies Axioms 4.1-4.5. In particular, ζ need
only be known on closed X-manifolds, and must be finite, must factorize on disjoint unions,
and must be reflection positive. Then the following are true:
(a) There exists a unitary functorial QFT (Definition 4.6), defined on the same class of
d-manifolds, with partition function ζ.
(b) Any two such QFTs are unitarily isomorphic.
(c) For such a QFT, let G denote the compact Hausdorff group implementing the maximal
unitary symmetry under which all sources in X are neutral. For any spatial manifold
B, the closed span of the set of states preparable by Euclidean X-manifolds with
boundary B is precisely the G-invariant subsector of the full Hilbert space of states
on B.
Stated less formally, parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 1.1 assert that:
Unitary quantum field theories are determined
by their closed-manifold partition function.
This is true both in the sense that a known unitary QFT can be reconstructed from
its closed-manifold partition function, and in the stronger sense that an abstract closed-
manifold partition function, known to be reflection positive, automatically produces a
unitary QFT. Thus, the closed-manifold partition function of a unitary QFT is not a coarse
invariant at all, as is sometimes stated; it is, instead, a complete invariant. Theorem 1.1
generalizes the perspective of Freed and Hopkins [66] from unitary invertible QFTs to
arbitrary unitary QFTs. Let us caution that our notion of unitary functorial QFT, given
in Definition 4.6, includes only compact QFTs, whose Euclidean closed-manifold partition
function is finite.
The class X of manifolds can be taken to include sources if one wants, but can always
be restricted to the bare minimum needed to define the QFT. For instance, we may take X
to consist of unadorned Riemannian manifolds with whatever tangential structure (orienta-
tion, spin structure, etc.) is required. The entire collection of Hilbert spaces and Euclidean
evolution operators are then uniquely reconstructed. One would only be forced to include
sources in order to distinguish theories, such as symmetry-protected topological (SPT) or
symmetry-enriched topological (SET) phases, which become trivial or indistinguishable
when one forgets the coupling to background fields.
As a consequence of Theorem 1.1, we are able to close a long-standing conjecture
of Friedan and Shenker [67] in the affirmative: unitary two-dimensional conformal field
theories (CFTs) are determined by their all-genus partition function.5 This is because, for
5Theorem 1.1 also likely resolves a number of conceptual descendants of Friedan and Shenker’s conjecture,
such as [68–71], though Theorem 1.1, as stated, does not apply to gravitationally anomalous theories, such
as two-dimensional chiral CFTs, which do not have a well-defined closed-manifold partition function.
– 5 –


## Page 8


a CFT, all correlation functions of local operators may be regarded as matrix elements of
Euclidean evolution operators via the state-operator correspondence. In the language of
the CFT bootstrap, Theorem 1.1 states that imposing reflection positivity of the closed-
manifold partition functions, for all manifolds at once, produces a complete set of bootstrap
constraints, in any dimension.
Theorem 1.1 also fits very well with the result [72] of
Wen and Wen, that certain (2 + 1)-dimensional topological orders (i.e., modular tensor
categories), with identical modular data, are nevertheless distinguished by their partition
functions on higher-genus mapping tori.
Applied to quantum gravity, parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 1.1 show that any breakdown
of Hilbert-space factorization is, ultimately, a red herring, in the sense of Coleman [19].
While the non-factorizing Hilbert spaces of states produced by cutting open the GPI are
not the full Hilbert spaces of any holographic theory, they can always be embedded in the
Hilbert spaces of an underlying boundary-local QFT, uniquely up to (non-unique) unitary
isomorphism. In a precise sense (see Section 9.4), choosing such an embedding is directly
analogous to projecting a non-factorizing partition function onto an α-sector.
Part (c) of Theorem 1.1 provides us with a natural reinterpretation of the original
breakdown of Hilbert-space factorization. Informally, part (c) states that a state can be
prepared by manifolds if and only if it is neutral under the largest possible symmetry group.
Equivalently, a state cannot be prepared by manifolds if and only if it is charged under a
symmetry group. This is true both when we include no sources beyond the manifold itself,
and also when we include any collection of sources we like. In particular, if we include
enough sources to break every symmetry of our QFT, then every state can be prepared by
manifolds with sources. Part (c) of Theorem 1.1 holds both for states on connected spatial
slices and also, crucially, for states on disconnected spatial slices.
Interpreted in unitary theories of quantum gravity, we learn that completeness of the
spectrum of charged states is not merely a necessary condition for ER = EPR, as shown
by Harlow [41], but is actually a sufficient condition as well. Thus, we show that:
Given unitarity, completeness of the
spectrum is equivalent to ER = EPR.
An important caveat, discussed in Section 11.3, is that the required notion of completeness
of the spectrum is completeness with respect to the total global symmetry group of the
microscopic holographic dual. This group may or may not be visible in terms of gauge
fields in a GPI or effective field theory (EFT) description we may have started with.
As a result, whenever partition-function factorization holds, but Hilbert-space factor-
ization breaks down, we obtain the following reinterpretation: the non-factorizing multi-
boundary Hilbert spaces of states preparable by the GPI are just the neutral subsector,

## Page 9


The expression (1.6) for the multi-boundary Hilbert spaces produced by the GPI is
directly analogous to the expression (1.3) for multi-boundary partition functions. In (1.3),
we write the multi-boundary partition functions as an average over α-sectors. More specif-
ically, it is a quenched average, meaning that we first multiply the partition functions for
each boundary and only take an average at the end. Similarly, (1.6) is a higher-categorical
form of quenched averaging, in that we first tensor the Hilbert spaces for each bound-
ary and only project onto G-invariant states at the end.6
This is a form of averaging
both in the straightforward sense that the projector onto G-invariants can be defined by
group-averaging, and in the more formal (but precise) sense discussed in Section 9.4. The
structure (1.6) was previously observed by Maxfield [73] in a beautiful toy example, re-
viewed in Section 6.1, which served as a major point of inspiration for this paper.
For the majority of this paper, we discuss the issue of Hilbert-space factorization
alone, assuming that partition-function factorization has already been resolved. Ultimately,
however, it is more natural to consider both issues at once, as we discuss in Section 10.3.
In such a case, with a nontrivial ensemble of α-sectors, we must generalize (1.6) to the
direct integral,

1.2
Overview of paper
At a high level, our paper is structured as follows. Sections 2, 3, 4, and 5 form a review
and formalization of the emerging axiomatic approach to the GPI, which provides the
formal context in which we prove Theorem 1.1. Its main output is the family of Hilbert
spaces HB of states prepared by manifolds, obtained using reflection positivity via a form
of the Osterwalder–Schrader (OS)/Gelfand–Naimark–Segal (GNS) construction. Section 6
illustrates Theorem 1.1 in a number of examples, including some low-dimensional theories
where we can directly prove Theorem 1.1 by hand.
Sections 7, 8, and 9 comprise the
technical core of this paper, in which we construct the baby universe category CBU and
use it to prove Theorem 1.1. Finally, with Theorem 1.1 proven, Section 10 includes stress
tests and extensions of our framework, and Section 11 concludes our discussion and points
towards future directions.
Let us now describe our proof of Theorem 1.1. As motivation, we briefly recall the de-
tails of Coleman’s argument [19] showing that the path integral over Euclidean wormholes
may be interpreted as producing a classical ensemble over α-sectors. From the modern per-
spective put forward by Marolf and Maxfield [25], this argument is based on understanding
the structure of the baby universe Hilbert space HBU, and the von Neumann algebra ABU
[60] acting on it. Both ABU and HBU are spanned by the collection of closed codimension-
one slices M of the bulk, with the algebra structure on ABU induced by disjoint union
6Let us contrast this operation with gauging the symmetry G, which can be viewed as a higher categorical
annealed average. First of all, when we gauge a symmetry, we project onto G invariants on each spatial
component independently. Secondly, we must also include twisted sectors, which has no analog in (1.6).
– 7 –


## Page 10


of manifolds. Linear combinations of geometric manifolds are subject to the Hamiltonian
constraint, which is implemented at the level of HBU by a quotient by null states.
The key observation of Coleman’s [19] is that the disjoint union of manifolds is commu-
tative, and so ABU is a commutative algebra. As a result, the quantum mechanics of baby
universe classicalizes. More precisely, ABU and HBU can be identified with the spaces of
bounded and square-integrable functions, respectively, on the spectrum Spec(ABU). This
spectrum is canonically equipped with a measure induced by the GPI (or, equivalently,
by the Hartle–Hawking no-boundary wavefunction [74]). The points of Spec(ABU) are,
precisely, the α-sectors discussed above. Each α-sector corresponds to a normal ∗-algebra
homomorphism ζα : ABU →C, which, when evaluated on a closed manifold M, produces
a factorizing partition function ζα(M).7
Our proof of Theorem 1.1 is a direct categorification of Coleman’s argument. While
ABU is the von Neumann algebra generated by closed codimension-one slices of the bulk,
the baby universe category CBU is the monoidal category generated by closed codimension-
two slices of the bulk. In more detail, the objects of CBU are given by formal direct sums
of bulk codimension-two closed manifolds, viewed as higher-codimension analogs of baby
universes. The morphisms in CBU are quantum superpositions of bulk codimension-one
bordisms, and thus CBU is a quantum bordism category, generalizing the bordism groups
of quantum gravity [59]. These bulk codimension-one bordisms should be viewed as partial
Cauchy slices of the bulk which evolve a codimension-two slice radially. Just as ABU is
an algebra under disjoint union of manifolds, the category CBU is monoidal, again under
disjoint union.
Both the morphisms and objects of CBU are subject to gravitational constraints. At
the level of morphisms, this is simply the bulk Hamiltonian constraint, which is imposed by
a quotient by null states as in the baby universe Hilbert space HBU. At the level of objects,
we have a higher-categorical version of the Hamiltonian constraint, enforcing that the bulk
radial evolution of codimension-two slices must be pure gauge. Formally, this constraint
is imposed by passing to an idempotent completion, as explained in Section 7.4. We view
the objects in CBU as possible horizons for ER bridges, and so the nontrivial objects in CBU
represent the most general sort of unbreakable flux which may flow through an ER bridge
and obstruct ER = EPR.
The specific categorical formalism we use in this paper is the framework of Cauchy
complete W*-categories, which provides a natural setting for categorical quantum mechan-
ics. We refer the reader to [75] for a review of the basic theory of W*-categories and a
clear development of their role as categorical analogs of Hilbert spaces. A W*-category
is an operator-algebraic category, all of whose endomorphism algebras are von Neumann
algebras (otherwise known as W*-algebras). W*-categories are Cauchy complete, in the
sense of [75], when they admit arbitrary orthogonal direct sums of objects as well as images
for every orthogonal projection operator. The baby universe category is a Cauchy com-
7Note that the measure space Spec(ABU) only admits a complete set of global points when it is discrete.
When Spec(ABU) is diffuse, it will not actually be possible to project onto a single α-sector, though it will
still be possible to restrict to subsets of α-sectors of non-zero measure.
– 8 –


## Page 11


plete W*-category, which is moreover a W*-tensor category8 under the monoidal structure
induced by disjoint union. We reassure readers unfamiliar with the general theory of von
Neumann algebras that CBU is, crucially, an atomic W*-category, meaning each endomor-
phism algebra is a direct sum of type I factors and nothing else.
Just as the von Neumann algebra ABU is commutative, the W*-tensor category CBU is
symmetric, again because the disjoint union of manifolds is commutative (in the strongest
sense). We saw that the existence of α-sectors arises from the basic fact that commuta-
tive von Neumann algebras always classicalize, reducing quantum mechanics to classical
probability theory. Analogously, symmetric W*-tensor categories C always classicalize as,
C = Rep(G),
(1.8)
for some compact group G.9
This is true, at least, when they satisfy an appropriate
finiteness condition, which we refer to as rigid generation and define in Section 9.1. We
refer to (1.8) as “classicalization,” as it has reduced the quantum mechanical possibility
of non-invertible symmetry to the representation theory of a classical (i.e., not quantum)
group.
The classicalization (1.8) of rigidly generated symmetric W*-tensor categories is de-
scribed in general by the framework of Tannakian reconstruction, and more specifically,
by the Doplicher–Roberts (DR) reconstruction theorem [76, 77]. The DR reconstruction
theorem forms the technical core of our argument, and Theorem 1.1 follows immediately
from DR reconstruction once we construct CBU and show that it is rigidly generated, by
leveraging the trace inequality of [62].
For readers familiar with the theory of Doplicher–Haag–Roberts (DHR) superselection
sectors [78–81] in algebraic QFT (see [82–89] for recent discussion), we note that our
main argument is simply an application of the philosophy of DHR superselection to the
superselection sectors of the GPI defined by fixing the boundary of a spatial slice. These
sectors break up into irreducible sectors, which are precisely the µ-sectors of [62, 63].
Thus, the superselection theory of boundaries of spatial slices in the GPI behaves like
DHR superselection theory in the stable range of d ≥4 spacetime dimensions.
In detail, this paper is organized as follows.
In Section 2, we describe the broad
philosophy behind the developing axiomatic approach to quantum gravity. In Section 3,
we describe the formal structures needed on the class X of manifolds, possibly with sources,
which serve as formal boundary conditions for the GPI. These structures are best phrased
in terms of a non-unital geometric bordism category, BordX
d , equipped with structures
encoding unitarity. In Section 4, we review the axioms placed on the partition function
ζ, following [62]. We also provide a definition for unitary functorial QFTs in the style of
Atiyah–Segal [90, 91], roughly following the approach of Kontsevich–Segal [92].
In Section 5, we use a form of the GNS construction to define Hilbert spaces of states

required to be Cauchy complete and have a simple tensor unit. When the condition on the tensor unit is
dropped, we will say we have a W*-multitensor category.
9Up to an important subtlety involving fermionic statistics, discussed in Section 9.

formally produced by manifolds, which we view as the natural Hilbert spaces obtained by
cutting open the GPI. This construction is known in the TQFT literature, independently,
as the universal construction of [93], and we refer to the constructed Hilbert spaces as the
universal Hilbert spaces. In this axiomatic framework, we state a precise formalization of
the ER = EPR conjecture [38–40], and set up the central questions of this paper: when do
the Hilbert spaces produced from the GPI factorize, and if they do not, what is going on?
In Section 6, we illustrate the answers provided by Theorem 1.1 in a number of explicit
examples, including in particular the toy model of Maxfield [73].
Our proof of Theorem 1.1 begins in earnest in Section 7, where we construct the
baby universe category CBU.
Our construction proceeds in a series of technical steps.
First, we define the pre-baby universe category Cpre
BU, the free linearization of BordX
d . The
pre-baby universe category admits a canonical family of representations on the universal
Hilbert spaces, and we define a W*-category CvN
BU by taking a categorical double commutant.
Applying standard results from Tomita–Takesaki theory, we reproduce the main results of
[62, 63] in our language: that CvN
BU is equipped with a faithful normal semifinite trace, which
is uniformly bounded below on projectors, proving that CvN
BU is atomic. We then construct
the baby universe category CBU itself by taking the W*-Cauchy completion of CvN
BU.
In Section 8, we equip the baby universe category CBU with the symmetric monoidal
fusion induced from the disjoint union of manifolds, and prove that CBU is rigidly generated.
Finally, in Section 9, we apply the DR reconstruction theorem to CBU, concluding our proof
of Theorem 1.1.
Let us note that this paper is intended, mainly, to address the issue of Hilbert-space fac-
torization, with applications in formal/mathematical QFT serving as an alternative source
of motivation and interpretation of our results. A follow-up paper [94] by Johnson-Freyd,
Reutter, and the first author of this paper will present our results in a mathematically
rigorous context, and explore the full categorical generality in which they apply. For a
preliminary report on this work in progress, see [95].
As our proof involves many moving pieces, we provide the following roadmap of the
various constructions and theorems we apply to go from a bordism category BordX

Schematically, the GPI computes the partition function ζ(M) by summing over all

2
An axiomatic approach to quantum gravity
As discussed in the Introduction, the basic structure of the GPI is the following: given a
bulk theory of gravity, we fix the boundary of spacetime and perform a path integral over
all possible bulks. Thus, schematically, the GPI is given by
ζ(M) def
=
Z
∂X=M
DX e−S(X),
(2.1)
where S(X) is the bulk action functional, and we always work in Euclidean signature. The
GPI runs over the space of bulk manifolds X with boundary M, as depicted in Figure 1.
Our convention for dimensions will be that d denotes the dimension of the manifold M held
fixed at the boundary of the gravitational spacetime, while the bulk is (d+1)-dimensional,
ignoring any possibly large internal dimensions.
Frequently, the bulk theory of gravity includes some dynamical fields ϕX, including a
dynamical metric tensor, which must also be integrated over in the GPI. For pedagogical
simplicity, we usually assume that we have chosen Dirichlet boundary conditions along
M, meaning that the bulk fields ϕX must restrict to a fixed choice ϕM of boundary fields
serving as a classical source. The matching of bulk fields with boundary sources is known
as the holographic dictionary, originally described by [96, 97] in the context of the anti-de
Sitter/conformal field theory (AdS/CFT) correspondence [98]. For more general boundary
conditions, we still use language adapted to Dirichlet boundary conditions, but strictly
speaking what we mean is that we turn on source fields for all possible deformations of the
boundary condition. We will frequently suppress explicit reference to fields, considering
them to be part of the structure of our manifolds, and always requiring isomorphisms of
manifolds to be field-preserving.
– 12 –


## Page 15


The GPI (2.1) is, in almost every case, only schematic. While it can sometimes be
evaluated in a semiclassical saddle-point approximation, there is frequently little hope
of fully evaluating the integral. The issues are more severe than the technical issues in
evaluating the QFT path integral, for multiple reasons, including the conformal mode
problem [99], the choice of integration contour [92, 99–102], and the measure for summing
over topologies [1, 16–21, 25, 103]. These issues arise, in large part, from the fact that there
is no full justification for the Euclidean GPI arising from Wick rotation from Lorentzian
signature.10 It has been conjectured [110] that the Euclidean GPI will only truly be justified
in a UV-complete theory of quantum gravity.
Nevertheless, whatever quantum gravity is, it should at least come with a notion of
allowed boundary conditions and a partition function ζ(M) for each allowed boundary
condition M. This is especially clear in the case of asymptotically AdS boundaries, where
we have a wealth of examples arising from the known landscape of AdS vacua of string
theory. As a result, a new, axiomatic approach to quantum gravity has been the subject
of increasing study in recent years [25, 33, 36, 60, 62–64]. In this approach, we simply
assume that the partition function ζ(M), ostensibly computed by the GPI, is known from
the outset. As a result, the GPI (2.1) is axiomatized away, just as the path integral of
QFT is axiomatized away in the Atiyah–Segal axiomatic approach to QFT.11
The currently developing axiomatic framework for quantum gravity is much more min-
imal than the Atiyah–Segal framework for QFT [90, 91]. In the Atiyah–Segal framework,
one axiomatizes not just the partition function, but also the Hilbert spaces and Euclidean
evolution operators of the QFT. These assemble into a unitary symmetric monoidal func-
tor out of a bordism category, as reviewed in Definition 4.6 below. Locality, motivated
by the cutting and gluing property of the QFT path integral, is fundamentally baked into
the Atiyah–Segal axioms. In contrast, for quantum gravity, we very emphatically do not
want to assume locality, and so the current approach is to simply axiomatize the partition
function ζ(M) by itself.
Technically, the emerging axiomatic framework consists of two main parts. The first
part is a class X of source manifolds M, or even just abstract “sources,” that can serve
as the input to the putative GPI. We discuss this class of source manifolds in Section 3.
The second part is the partition function ζ(M) itself, which is taken to satisfy a list of
axioms laid out in Section 4 (possibly dropping multiplicativity, i.e., Axiom 4.3).
The
required properties of the class X of source manifolds are actually identical to those for a
d-dimensional QFT, and thus it makes sense to ask whether a given gravitational partition
function ζ arises from a d-dimensional QFT or not. When it does, we say that the quantum
theory of gravity is holographic, which is, crucially, not taken as an assumption in this paper.
Let us note that the axioms for the gravitational partition function are strictly weaker
than those of QFT, in that the partition function of any QFT will automatically satisfy
them.
Thus, there is a formal sense, discussed in more detail below, in which any d-
dimensional QFT can be viewed as a holographic quantum theory of gravity in (d + 1)
10See [102, 104–109] for more on the GPI in Lorentzian signature.
11The path integral is still quite useful in constructing and/or motivating examples of QFTs, and the
GPI can likely play an analogous role.
– 13 –


## Page 16


dimensions. From this perspective, we view the state produced in a QFT by evolution
over some manifold as a state in which the bulk simply is that manifold, at least before
imposing the bulk Hamiltonian constraint. Throughout, we will frequently switch back
and forth between the QFT perspective, in which we are considering a candidate partition
function for a d-dimensional QFT, and the gravitational perspective, in which we view the
partition function and resulting quantum theory as arising from a (d+1)-dimensional GPI.
3
Bordism categories of source manifolds
In this section, we outline the formal properties we require of the class X of source mani-
folds. These formal properties are best expressed as structures on the geometric bordism
category BordX
d . Geometric bordism categories have been studied previously [91, 92, 111–
115], with the approaches of Stolz–Teichner [114] and Kontsevich–Segal [92] providing the
most direct motivation for our approach in this paper. The most important structure we
require on BordX
d is what we call a unitary structure, as defined in Section 3.2, and as
motivated by [116–118].
Ultimately, nothing essential in our framework requires the source category to have
anything to do with bordism. The necessary structures are purely categorical, and once
we have the category BordX
d in hand, with the structures we discuss, we are free to forget
how it was built. This will be shown explicitly in the follow-up work [94] of Johnson-Freyd,
Reutter, and the first author of this paper.
3.1
Source data and locality
To start, fix some class X of local source data on d-dimensional manifolds. This source
data may include topological data such as an orientation or a spin structure, as well as
continuous data, such as background fields of various kinds or marked loci representing
operator insertions. One field of particular importance is a fixed Riemannian metric, which
sources the bulk gravitational field. To fix terminology, we say that a choice of source data
on a d-manifold is an X-structure, and a d-manifold M equipped with an X-structure is
an X-manifold. Two X-manifolds are considered to be the same when they are related by
an X-isomorphism, i.e., an X-preserving diffeomorphism. For instance, when X includes a
Riemannian metric, X-isomorphisms must be isometries.
Formally, the class X is a sheaf on the site of smooth d-manifolds and local diffeomor-
phisms [119]. The condition that X be a sheaf encodes the locality of the source data, as
it asserts that we may cut and glue X-structures coordinate patch by coordinate patch.12
We restrict to local source data because our goal is to probe locality; if our sources were
not local, it would be impossible to ask the question. We assume that X is equipped with
a topology, which we assume to be separable on compact d-manifolds.13
In this paper, we will need to study both d-dimensional and (d −1)-dimensional man-
ifolds equipped with X-structures. A standard issue is that, since X-structures are only
12In other words, an X-structure is something which transforms as an X-structure.
13Separability holds for any physically reasonable choice of source data, including spaces of finitely (or
even countably) many smooth fields, or configuration spaces of marked loci.

X-manifold in which B is embedded as a co-oriented, codimension-one submanifold. We

Physically, we view the manifold B as a spatial slice of the ambient d-dimensional
source spacetime.
Saying that we only have a germ means that we identify any two
spacetimes if they are isomorphic in a neighborhood of B via an isomorphism which acts
trivially on B; thus, a germ only remembers an infinitesimal piece of spacetime around
B. The co-orientation of B in spacetime means that we have chosen an orientation for
its normal bundle, which physically serves as the arrow of time.15

(d −1)-dimensional X-manifolds are required to preserve the arrow of time.

from the incoming boundary B1 to the outgoing boundary B2, both in the context of QFT
and in the GPI. Given X-bordisms

The collections of (d−1)-dimensional X-manifolds and d-dimensional X-bordisms form
the objects and morphisms of the X-bordism category, BordX
d . For most physically relevant
choices of source data X, the bordism category BordX
d will be non-unital, meaning that it
will lack identity morphisms. This is because all non-empty bordisms are required to have
some finite Euclidean duration, as measured by some invariant like the distance between
the boundaries or the total volume, which can only increase under composition.
The main exception is when the class X is topological. Formally, this means that the
sheaf X is locally constant, in that specifying an X-structure in a small neighborhood of any
point is enough to uniquely specify an X-structure in any contractible region containing
that point. A class of topological source data X is the same thing [120, 122–124] as a
tangential structure in the sense of [66, 125]. When X is topological, the bordism category
BordX
d is unital.
This is because, with no way to measure the duration of a bordism,
cylinders can serve as identity morphisms.
Working with non-unital categories is a bit difficult because they do not have a notion
of categorical isomorphism.
One approach, taken for instance in [114], is to adjoin all
X-isomorphisms of (d −1)-dimensional manifolds as additional morphisms in BordX
d . The
composition of an X-isomorphism with a bordism is defined by twisting the appropriate
boundary by the given isomorphism. This approach produces a unital category, but not a
category whose morphisms can always be viewed as finite-length bordisms.
Our approach will be different, following a suggestion made by Kontsevich and Segal in
[92].16 For us, the category BordX
d will be genuinely non-unital, but we will use the notion
of X-isomorphism in place of categorical isomorphism whenever we consider whether two
objects or morphisms of BordX
d are the same.17
As a result, in the various categorical

By abuse of notation, we will still use u ◦N or N ◦u to denote the composition of an
X-isomorphism u with a bordism N, which makes sense even though X-automorphisms
are not morphisms in our category BordX
d .
The bordism category BordX
d allows us to cut X-manifolds along spatial slices, which
is sufficient to encode locality in time. Let us now discuss locality in space. If we wanted
to discuss full spatial locality, we should continue cutting our spatial slices in codimension-
two, codimension-three, and so on, until we could discuss arbitrarily small neighborhoods
of each spacetime point. In the topological case, this extended cutting is formalized by the
definition of bordism d-categories [122, 126], and there is no obstruction in defining geo-
metric bordism d-categories. However, in geometric QFTs, there are severe analytic issues
related to cutting in higher codimension, including the expected Type III von Neumann
algebras which appear once one cuts a spatial slice along a codimension-two interface.
For our goal of understanding Hilbert-space factorization, we so not need to address
these analytic issues. Instead, we restrict ourselves to a trivial-seeming special case: cutting
a disjoint spatial slice B1 ⊔B2 along the empty codimension-two interface separating B1
from B2.
While this cut may not be so interesting in QFT, it is highly nontrivial in
quantum gravity. This is because we can have a nontrivial codimension-two cut of a bulk
spatial slice which does not extend out to the boundary. For instance, the horizon, or
cross-section, of an ER bridge connecting B1 and B2 through the bulk is precisely such a
cut.
Categorically, the possibility of cutting a disjoint spatial manifold B1⊔B2 into its com-
ponents is encoded in the symmetric monoidal structure on the bordism category BordX
d .
We recall that a symmetric monoidal structure on a category C is a unital fusion operation,
⊗: C × C →C,

Some examples of topological bordism categories are the categories BordO

a Riemannian analog, where, in addition to the tangential structure, we also require the
choice of a Riemannian metric. Other examples of source data include background scalar
isomorphisms of manifolds, but also h-cobordisms. See [118] for further discussion.

3.2
Unitary structures
In this paper, our goal is to study unitary quantum systems, whose states live in Hilbert
spaces and admit probabilistic interpretations via the Born rule. In order to make sense
of unitarity, and its Euclidean avatar, reflection positivity, the class X of source manifolds
must be equipped with additional structure. Parts of this structure were discussed recently
in [121] in the context of the GPI. Our approach is directly motivated by [66, 116–118, 127].
As motivation, let us recall the Osterwalder–Schrader (OS)/Wightman approach [128–
131] to axiomatic QFT. In this approach, one starts with Euclidean correlation functions
⟨−⟩Ωof fields ϕ(τ, ⃗x) on Rd, ostensibly computed in some vacuum state |Ω⟩.
In order
to turn these Euclidean correlation functions into an inner product, one needs the OS
conjugation map Θ, which acts on fields by
Θ : ϕ(τ, ⃗x) 7→ϕ†(−τ, ⃗x),
(3.5)
as depicted in Figure 4. Given a collection ϕ of fields supported on the lower half-space,
one defines formal states |ϕΩ⟩, with inner product defined by,
⟨ϕ1Ω|ϕ2Ω⟩def
= ⟨Θ(ϕ1)ϕ2⟩Ω.
(3.6)
It is only at this stage, once OS conjugation has been applied, that one asks for reflection
positivity.
Thus, we need an OS conjugation map on the bordism category BordX

At a technical level, what we need is a †-structure, or dagger-

1 ◦M2 represents their wavefunction overlap, as depicted in Figure 5. Moreover,
we demand that every X-isomorphism is unitary, in that its image under † is its inverse.
To find a †-structure, let us first note that the bordism category BordX

because it is not typically the identity on objects. For example, if an oriented manifold B ∈

between B and B∨as objects of BordSO

an isomorphism B →B∨must also reverse the orientation of space in order to preserve
the orientation of spacetime. As a result, we cannot use the dual functor (−)∨, on its own,
as an analog of OS conjugation.
While not a †-structure, the functor (−)∨is still quite useful. In fact, the object B∨is
a non-unital version of a dual object for B. This means that we have a family of canonical

## Page 22


defined by reversing the arrow of time on the boundary component B of the bordism N.
Thus, the symmetric monoidal category BordX
d is rigid monoidal, in a non-unital sense,
meaning that each object admits a dual.
It may come as a surprise to readers familiar with TQFT that BordX
d is rigid, as
dualizability of objects in topological bordism categories is directly connected to the finite
dimensionality of Hilbert spaces in TQFT. This is because one may use duals to turn the
identity map X →X of any object into a coevaluation map 1 →X ⊗X∨, which is only
possible in finite dimensional vector spaces.
In contrast, in the non-unital rigid monoidal category BordX
d , we cannot define coeval-
uation maps because we do not have identity morphisms in the first place. Nevertheless, we
may still turn any bordism A →B of finite length into a bordism ∅→B⊔A∨. For compar-
ison, consider the category HilbHS, whose objects are Hilbert spaces and whose morphisms
are Hilbert–Schmidt operators. Any Hilbert–Schmidt operator b
O : H1 →H2 can be turned
into a state |O⟩∈H2 ⊗H∨
1 ; infinite dimensionality causes no issues here, because the iden-
tity operator on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space is never Hilbert–Schmidt. For this
reason, finite length bordisms behave in many ways like Hilbert–Schmidt operators.18 This
is the essential analytic reason why we do not include identity morphisms in the category
BordX
d .

The conjugate functor (−) and the Hermitian structure ρB are required to satisfy a
collection of coherence axioms, which we discuss in detail in Appendix A. Specifically,

Equivalently (for our

these equivalent structures as a unitary structure, and a functor which preserves them as
a unitary functor.
Let us spell out the additional constraints which enter into the definition of a unitary
structure. First, we require an additional family of coherence isomorphisms B
∨∼= B∨.
For us, these isomorphisms will simply be identity maps, since reversing the arrow of time
and conjugating sources commute on the nose. Via these isomorphisms, we obtain what is

A unitary structure on BordX
d is a non-linear analog of a
balanced unitary dual functor (UDF) on a unitary tensor category [127]. We say that a
bordism category BordX
d equipped with a unitary structure is a unitary bordism category.
To illustrate the physical meaning of the twist θB, consider the following example.
Example 3.2. Let BordSpin
d
denote the topological bordism category of d-manifolds equipped
with spin structures. As discussed in [66, 116, 117], the spin bordism category BordSpin
d
admits a canonical unitary structure with the following property: for every spin (d −1)-
manifold, the twist automorphism θB : B →B is the 2π rotation spin diffeomorphism,
which acts as the identity on the points of B and acts on spin frames as a 2π rotation.
As illustrated by Example 3.2, the twist automorphism θB corresponds, physically, to
a 2π rotation of B.19 The condition θ2
B = idB is thus the statement that a 4π rotation
must act trivially, which is physically motivated by the fact that our spatial manifolds are
not embedded anywhere, and thus cannot have anyonic behavior under rotation. We say
that a unitary bordism category BordX
d is bosonic if θB = idB for all objects B, and is
fermionic otherwise. This interpretation of θB is justified by the spin-statistics theorem for
unitary functorial QFTs [66, 116, 117], which asserts that θB (measuring spin) must map
to fermion parity (−1)F (measuring statistics) in any unitary functorial QFT (Definition
4.6).
Example 3.2 illustrates an additional important point: equipping the bordism cate-
gory BordX
d with a unitary structure is a nontrivial step, which may involve physically
meaningful choices. To see this, consider the example of [116], given by the spin bordism
category BordSpin
1
in d = 1 dimensions. Now, note that a spin structure on a 1-manifold is
precisely the same data as an orientation and a separate Z2 gauge bundle. This is because
an orientation on a 1-manifold already trivializes the tangent bundle, and removing the

While equipping BordX
d with a unitary structure is nontrivial in general, at least in the
topological case BordX
d admits a canonical unitary structure whenever X is a stable tangen-
tial structure, meaning, some structure on the stabilized tangent bundle [66, 118]. Stable
tangential structures include, for instance: orientations, spin structures, pin structures,
and background gauge fields for any finite symmetry. While the analog of this canonical
unitary structure is not clear in the geometric case, we can use the same canonical unitary
structure if X is the class of Riemannian manifolds equipped, additionally, with some sta-
ble tangential structure. Moreover, many choices of geometric source data seem to admit
natural unitary structures. For instance, a complex metric is taken by (−) to its complex
conjugate [92].
3.2.1
Restrictions on germs and sources
In order for a geometric bordism category BordX
d to admit a unitary structure, we are forced
to place a few restrictions on the germs of spacetime and the types of sources we allow.
The first, discussed above, is that we must restrict to germs which admit a time-reversal
symmetry that fixes the spatial slice, so that every object B ∈BordX
d is equipped with a
Hermitian structure ρB : B →B
∨. Thankfully, this restriction is also physically natural,
as this is the only case in which we expect quantum states to form a Hilbert space in any
case [92]. We must also demand that the source fields turned on around the spatial slices
are invariant under (−). For instance, a complex scalar field may only take real values on
the fixed spatial slice, and must be taken to its complex conjugate under time reversal.
For the sake of simplicity, we make the additional assumption that the germs of space-
time admit an infinitesimal time translation symmetry. In particular, we assume the germ
of a metric around an object B ∈BordX
d takes the form,
ds2 = NB(⃗x)2dτ 2 + gB
ij(⃗x)dxidxj,
(3.21)
up to diffeomorphism, for some metric gB
ij and lapse function NB on B.
– 24 –


## Page 27


This restriction on germs ensures that each object B ∈BordX
d admits a one-parameter
semigroup of self-adjoint self-bordisms, given by cylinders,
CB(β) = [0, β] × B,
CB(β1) ◦CB(β2) = CB(β1 + β2),
CB(β)† = CB(β),
(3.22)
as used in [62, 63]. Here, β > 0 denotes the parameter controlling the semigroup. Note
that CB(β) is only a Riemannian product when the lapse NB is constant, in which case
we will always rescale τ to set NB = 1 and take β to denote the actual geometric length of
CB(β). In a unitary QFT, the evolution operator along CB(β) will be e−β b
HB, where bHB
is the Hamiltonian operator on B associated to the lapse function NB. In the topological
case, we can drop the parameter β, as the cylinders of different length are all isomorphic
and serve as identity bordisms.
While the restriction to germs with a time-reversal symmetry is structurally important,
the restriction to germs with a time-translation symmetry is essentially cosmetic (though
necessary for defining a Hamiltonian operator). Without this restriction, we would replace
the semigroup (3.22) with the filtered category of bordisms into B, as was used in [92] to
define what they called the continuity property. This generalization will play a central role
in the follow-up work [94, 95], in terms of the firmness property (in the sense of [132]) of
Riemannian bordism categories.
The second structural restriction concerns fermionic sources.
The axioms defining
a unitary structure on BordX
d demand that the twist automorphisms θB form a natural
automorphism,
θ : idBordX
d ⇒idBordX
d ,
(3.23)
of the identity functor on BordX
d . Concretely, this means that the twists commute with all
bordisms,
θB ◦N = N ◦θA,
∀N : A →B.
(3.24)
As a result, in order for BordX
d to admit a unitary structure, the class X of source fields
cannot include any fermionic fields or operator insertions, meaning sources which transform
nontrivially under θB.
To avoid confusion, we emphasize that BordX
d can still include source fields, such as spin
structures or spinc gauge fields, which allow the presence of fermionic degrees of freedom in
a QFT defined on X-manifolds. As an illustrative example, note that while a background
spinc gauge field Aµ couples to dynamical spinor fields ψ, this interaction takes the form
ψAµγµψ, so Aµ only couples to the fermion bilinear ψγµψ, a bosonic operator. What we
do not allow are fields, such as background spinor fields, which would couple directly to
fermionic operators.
Though structurally important to our construction throughout most of this paper,
there is a clear fix, which we outline in Section 10.2. The issue with fermionic sources
is not really unitarity, but symmetric monoidality: fermionic sources must anticommute
at spacelike separation, rather than commute. For comparison, consider the symmetric
monoidal category sVec of super-vector spaces, whose symmetry is defined by the Koszul
sign rule. Importantly, the morphisms in sVec are only the bosonic linear maps. If we tried
– 25 –


## Page 28


to include fermionic linear maps as morphisms in sVec, while still using the Koszul sign
rule, we would obtain a category which is not symmetric monoidal in the usual sense.20

3.3
The bordism group
We close our discussion of the source category BordX
d by describing an important invariant:
the bordism group,
ΩX
d−1
def
= π0(BordX
d ),
(3.34)
whose elements are equivalence classes [B] of (d−1)-dimensional X-manifolds. In ΩX
d−1, we
have [B1] = [B2] whenever B1 and B2 are X-bordant, meaning connected by any X-bordism
whatsoever. At least with our restrictions on germs, X-bordism is an equivalence relation:
it is reflexive due to the existence of cylinders, it is symmetric via the †-structure, and it is
transitive due to composition of bordisms. The symmetric monoidal structure on BordX
d
endows ΩX
d−1 with the structure of an abelian group,
[B1] + [B2] = [B1 ⊔B2],

We will see that the bordism group provides a classical obstruction to ER = EPR
for any (non-zero) theory defined on smooth X-manifolds, as anticipated in [133]. This
classical obstruction is a shadow of the full obstruction to ER = EPR described by the
baby universe category CBU, and ultimately we obtain a grading of CBU by bordism classes.
In order to avoid the obstruction arising from bordism, one would need to generalize BordX
d
to a category of bordisms with singularities [59], as discussed for instance in [122]. All of
our constructions and main results extend to bordisms with singularities without significant
modification.
4
Axioms for the partition function
Having described our requirements on the sources of the GPI, we now describe our require-
ments on the partition function ζ, ostensibly computed by the GPI (though the axioms can
apply equally well regardless of how ζ was computed). We then compare these minimal
axioms for ζ to an axiomatization of unitary QFT in the style of Atiyah–Segal.
4.1
Abstract partition functions
Fix a unitary bordism category BordX
d . The first axiom specifies that ζ(M) is a known,
finite number for all closed d-dimensional X-manifolds M.
Axiom 4.1 (Finiteness). For each closed d-dimensional source manifold M, we are given
a finite number ζ(M) ∈C. We assume that ζ(M) is invariant under X-isomorphism.
– 28 –


## Page 31


Gravitationally, our assumption that ζ(M) is finite for closed manifolds M may be moti-
vated by the observations of [134] related to the finiteness of gravitational amplitudes. In
QFT, the finiteness of ζ(M) implies that a QFT with partition function ζ must be compact,
as discussed below.
We assume that the partition function ζ(M) satisfies the following two kinematic
conditions.
Axiom 4.2 (Reality). We assume that ζ takes conjugation of source manifolds to complex
conjugation, meaning that ζ(M) = ζ(M) for all closed M.
Axiom 4.3 (Multiplicativity). We assume that ζ is multiplicative on disjoint unions, so
that we have ζ(M1 ⊔M2) = ζ(M1)ζ(M2) for any two source manifolds M1, M2. Moreover,
we assume that ζ(∅) = 1.
Taken together, Axioms 4.1, 4.2, and 4.3 assert that ζ defines a ∗-monoid homomorphism
from the monoid of closed d-dimensional X-manifolds under disjoint union to the complex
numbers under multiplication.
Axiom 4.2 may be motivated in quantum gravity from the fact that CPT must be a
gauge symmetry in the bulk [110], in the sense that conjugating the boundary of the GPI
must produce a complex-conjugate partition function.
Axiom 4.3, though unnatural from the perspective of the GPI, is motivated by the
assumption that we have already resolved any breakdown of partition-function factoriza-
tion, and is made for technical simplicity (see Section 10.3 for a sketch of how Axiom 4.3
might be dropped). The resolution of partition-function factorization might be achieved
by projecting onto an α-sector, or, equivalently, by finely tuning our given GPI to ensure
partition-function factorization [135–138]. Alternatively, we could simply assume we are
working with a UV-complete theory of quantum gravity, such as string theory, in which
the issue of partition-function factorization never arises in the first place [26, 139].22
In addition to satisfying Axioms 4.1-4.3, we expect the partition function to depend
continuously on sources, including the choice of Riemannian metric. In [62], a very mild
continuity assumption was made, requiring only continuity in the length of an embedded
cylinder. We make a stronger continuity assumption, as it seems to be required for a few
auxiliary results (though, interestingly, not for the proof of our main theorem).23
Axiom 4.4 (Continuity). We assume that ζ(M) depends continuously on sources in our
chosen topology on the space of X-structures on M.
Combining Axiom 4.4 with our assumption that ζ is invariant under isomorphisms of X-
manifolds, we could alternatively say that ζ is a continuous function on the moduli space
of closed d-dimensional X-manifolds. We make no assumptions about the behavior of ζ
at the boundaries of moduli space where source fields such as the metric degenerate (in
contrast to [67]). Note that Axiom 4.4 is vacuous when X is topological.
22This last approach, while justified given a UV-complete theory, also likely renders the whole question
of Hilbert-space factorization moot.
23We thank Tom Banks for encouraging us to make this stronger continuity assumption.
– 29 –


## Page 32


The four axioms already described are kinematic, and the space of partition functions
that satisfy them is not meaningfully smaller than the space of all possible functions ζ(M).
This is not the case for our last axiom: reflection positivity, the Euclidean avatar of unitar-
ity. As discussed above, reflection positivity is required in order for our partition function
to have a consistent interpretation in terms of wavefunction overlaps. From the perspective
of the GPI, reflection positivity is not at all obvious [25, 61, 62, 64, 103, 140–144]. Nev-
ertheless, circumstantially, the GPI seems to behave consistently with unitarity in more
ways than may have been naively expected, including giving an accurate count of black
hole microstates and a derivation of the Page curve, as discussed in the Introduction.
To formulate reflection positivity, consider any (d −1)-manifold B ∈BordX
d , and two
bordisms from the empty set into B.
M1, M2 ∈BordX
d (∅→B).
(4.1)

ifold B. As discussed in Section 3.2, the closed manifold corresponding to the wavefunction
overlap of those two states is given by,
⟨M1|M2⟩Bord = M†

(4.2)
as depicted above in Figure 5. Thus, knowing nothing about any underlying QFT, we
already know that the wavefunction overlap must be given by,
⟨M1|M2⟩= ζ

This expression is analogous to the inner product derived from Euclidean correlation func-

Axiom 4.5 (Reflection Positivity). For all B ∈BordX
d , all finite collections of manifolds

A simple consequence of reflection positivity is that the partition function on a double,
ζ(M†◦M), must be positive. However, in general, we need to consider arbitrary sesquilinear
combinations, for the simple fact that a Hermitian matrix with positive entries on the
diagonal is not necessarily positive semidefinite. The only general exception is when the
matrix is known to be rank-one (as will be the case for invertible theories), in which case
positivity of ζ on doubles is sufficient [66].
Compared to Axioms 4.1-4.4, which are purely kinematic and easy to satisfy, reflection
positivity is an extremely strong assumption about ζ.
For any fixed B and any finite
– 30 –


## Page 33


collection of states, reflection positivity is a finite system of inequalities. However, reflection
positivity for all manifolds and spatial slicings at once is an infinite system of inequalities.
As a result, reflection positivity can cut the space of partition functions down dramatically,
as is familiar in the context of the bootstrap.
We note that reflection positivity is particularly constraining when applied to discon-
nected spatial slices. For instance, applying reflection positivity to disconnected spatial
slices directly implies integrality of the degeneracies of every energy level, which is some-
times taken as an additional assumption in the context of the bootstrap. We will see this
in practice in the example discussed in Section 6.1, based on [25, 62, 63, 73]. We will see it
again at the core of the proof of the DR reconstruction theorem [76, 77], and thus also at
the core of our proof of Theorem 1.1. If one is looking for a driving force behind Theorem
1.1, it is reflection positivity applied to disconnected spatial slices, which is precisely also
where the question of Hilbert-space factorization arises in the first place.
4.2
Functorial quantum field theories
Let us now give our working definition of a unitary functorial QFT, in the spirit of Atiyah
and Segal. Our definition takes its most direct inspiration from the approach of Kontsevich
and Segal [92], though with the restriction (discussed in Section 3.2.1) to spatial slices with
infinitesimal time-reversal and time-translation symmetries.
Definition 4.6. A unitary quantum field theory of dimension d is a non-degenerate, uni-
tary, symmetric monoidal functor
BordX

from a unitary bordism category to the category of super-Hilbert spaces.24
We have taken the target of our QFTs to be sHilb, in order to accommodate fermionic
statistics. By the spin-statistics theorem, every unitary QFT on a bosonic bordism category
factors through the subcategory Hilb ⊂sHilb of purely bosonic Hilbert spaces, and so for
bosonic theories we could take the target to be Hilb with no loss of generality. We will
suppress the prefix “super” in super-Hilbert space for the remainder of this section.

We have thus shown that four of our axioms for the partition function (namely, Axioms
4.1, 4.2, 4.3, and 4.5) follow from Definition 4.6, and so our axioms for the partition function
are, almost, strictly weaker than our axioms for a unitary QFT. We have intentionally
neglected to assume that the evolution operators b
N depend continuously on source fields,
besides the strong convergence bCB(β) →1 as β →0. Remarkably, continuity of ζ in sources
actually implies continuity of b
N in sources (in the weak-∗topology). This will follow as
a consequence of Theorem 1.1, as the unique reconstructed QFT has this property by
Proposition B.5, and our proof of Theorem 1.1 does not require us to assume any continuity
of our QFT besides the continuity of ζ.
Definition 4.6 is an axiomatization of what is typically called a compact QFT in the
physics literature. The exact definition of a compact QFT varies, but the standard require-
ments include a finite thermal partition function when quantized on a compact spatial man-
ifold (this implies that the Hamiltonian on a compact spatial manifold has a discrete energy
spectrum). Definition 4.6 requires the partition function on every compact Euclidean man-
ifold to be finite, which includes the thermal partition functions as a special case. Thus,
we exclude theories with continuous spectra, such as the Liouville CFT in d = 2, as well as
theories with unbounded Hagedorn growth of the spectrum. We also exclude non-compact
TQFTs, such as the BF theories arising from the so-called “flat gauging” of continuous
gauge groups (see, e.g., [145, 146]).
One may worry that the closed-manifold partition function of a physical QFT is typ-
ically UV divergent, and thus requires regularization and renormalization. Our notion of

QFT axiomatizes the result of having performed this renormalization in some fixed choice

defining fermion parity in the Ramond sector of the superstring worldsheet).
5
Spaces of gravitational states
We now turn to the problem of building Hilbert spaces HB of states prepared by manifolds
with boundary B, given only a closed-manifold partition function ζ on a unitary bor-
dism category BordX
d . Viewed gravitationally, the Hilbert spaces HB consist of all states
preparable by cutting open the GPI. If we have included sources for all degrees of freedom
in the bulk EFT, then this is also precisely the set of states obtained by allowing effective
field theory (EFT) degrees of freedom to evolve and interact gravitationally.
Pieces of this construction have appeared previously in the recent quantum gravity
literature [25, 33, 36, 60, 62–64], as well as in the formal TQFT literature under the name
of the universal construction [93] (see also [147–149]), which we adopt here.
The uni-
versal construction is a form of the standard Gelfand–Naimark–Segal (GNS) construction
[150, 151], as used in the OS/Wightman approach to axiomatic QFT. In the GNS con-
struction, one starts with a state on a ∗-algebra, and uses the state to build a Hilbert space
representation of formally defined states.
In a precise sense, the abstract partition function ζ defines a state (in fact, a tra-
cial state) on the unitary bordism category BordX
d , and the universal construction is the
corresponding form of the GNS construction.28
Thus, we will use ζ to build a formal,
non-degenerate Hilbert space representation of BordX
d , or in other words, a non-degenerate
unitary functor,
BordX
d →Hilb,
(5.1)
which is, almost, a unitary QFT. However, as we discuss in Section 5.2, the universal
construction fails to define a QFT by failing to produce tensor-factorized Hilbert spaces
on disconnected spatial manifolds. This failure of the universal construction to define a
unitary QFT leads to the central question of study in this paper.

Next, the universal Hilbert space HB is defined to be the Cauchy completion of the

with respect to the seminorm induced by the inner product (5.3).
Taking this Cauchy completion automatically includes the quotient by the subspace of null
states, which forms the closure of the origin in the non-Hausdorff topology induced by
the seminorm. This Cauchy completion is automatically a Hilbert space, by construction.
Note that, modulo null states, any components of M which are not connected to B may
be replaced by their partition function using the multiplicativity of ζ.
Let us comment on the interpretation of the state |M⟩. In QFT, the state |M⟩is the
state prepared by a Euclidean path integral over M. In contrast, gravitationally, |M⟩rep-
resents the state prepared by a GPI with M held fixed in the Euclidean past, as illustrated
in Figure 10. But, since bulk time evolution in quantum gravity is pure gauge, one could
justifiably say that the state |M⟩is a state where M is the bulk geometry.29 Moreover,
the source fields correspond holographically to dynamical fields in the bulk, so the config-
uration of source fields on M specifies the precise configuration of the corresponding bulk
fields. Note that this holographic interpretation is always available, regardless of how ζ
was computed. Thus, any QFT can formally be regarded as a theory of quantum gravity,
in the somewhat tautological sense that it has a subspace of quantum states parametrized
by manifolds.
29At least in the sense of a field-basis eigenstate, if not in the sense of a coherent state.

automatically solve the bulk momentum constraint.30 However, we have not taken into
account the bulk Hamiltonian constraint, so one should view Hpre
B
as a kinematic vector
space where we can unambiguously describe the geometry and topology of the bulk spatial
slice without having to worry about bulk time evolution.
In contrast, HB is a Hilbert space of physical states arising from projecting states
in the kinematic vector space Hpre
B
onto the space of solutions to the non-perturbative
Hamiltonian constraint. This projection takes the form of the quotient by null states.31 As
a result, the states in HB generically do not have unique expressions as linear combinations
of manifolds. Gravitationally, this corresponds to the fact that the geometry [153–155]
and topology [156] of a bulk Cauchy slice are not gauge invariant after the Hamiltonian
constraint is imposed. One can ask how well the inner product on HB distinguishes the
states |M⟩for different M, in order to get a diagnostic of how close the bulk may be to
semiclassical [157–162].
A natural worry is that the universal Hilbert spaces we have constructed are too big to
possibly be the Hilbert spaces of a QFT, as we have states in HB for every source manifold
M with boundary B. However, as shown in Appendix B.2, the universal Hilbert spaces HB
are always separable, and the states |M⟩∈HB vary continuously as a function of sources.
These results are direct consequences of continuity of ζ and our assumption that the spaces
of sources on compact manifolds are seperable.

However, the first part of monoidality, namely, Hilbert-space factorization, fails. The
reason is that, unlike the states of a local QFT, the states in the universal construction
are not prepared in a local way. The main issue is that we can have connected d-manifolds
M whose boundary is disjoint, ∂M = B1 ⊔B2. Viewed gravitationally, such states corre-
spond to spatial wormholes, or Einstein–Rosen (ER) bridges. In general, we only have a
comparison map
⊔: HB1 ⊗HB2 →HB1⊔B2,
|M1⟩⊗|M2⟩7→|M1 ⊔M2⟩,
(5.12)
between the tensor products of universal Hilbert spaces and universal Hilbert spaces on
the disjoint union. Multiplicativity implies that (5.12) is an isometric embedding, but it
has no reason to be surjective in general.
Instead, the maps (5.12), together with the
Hartle–Hawking state |∅⟩∈HBU, endow the universal construction with a lax symmetric
monoidal structure [93], which is better than nothing but not enough to qualify as a local
QFT.
As discussed in the Introduction, there is a proposal, in the context of quantum grav-
ity, for how ER bridges could fit inside a tensor product Hilbert space. This proposal goes
by the name of ER = EPR [38–40], and asserts that, modulo the non-perturbative Hamil-
tonian constraint [156], ER bridges should all be equivalent to entangled superpositions of
disconnected states. The most well-studied example is the state of the AdS-Schwarzschild
wormhole, which has been identified [38] with the thermofield double state,
|TFDβ⟩=

in the holographically dual CFT quantized on Sd−1 ⊔Sd−1. In our language, the argu-

graphic CFT, the state |CSd−1(β/2)⟩is precisely the thermofield double state |TFDβ⟩, as it
is obtained by partially dualizing the Euclidean evolution operator e−β b
HSd−1/2 into a state.
In our framework, we can give an entirely rigorous definition of the ER = EPR proposal
as follows.
– 38 –


## Page 41


Definition 5.1. Let ζ be a partition function on a unitary bordism category BordX
d ,
satisfying Axioms 4.1-4.5. We say ER = EPR holds for X and ζ if the canonical isometric
map,
⊔: HB1 ⊗HB2 →HB1⊔B2
(5.14)
is an isomorphism for all B1, B2.32
When ER = EPR holds, every ER bridge lies in the subspace spanned by superpositions
of disconnected states, and may therefore be written as an entangled superposition of
disconnected states modulo null states. Let us emphasize that ER = EPR is a property
of both the partition function ζ and the class X. This is because ER = EPR, requiring
a complete set of one-sided states, depends sensitively on which one-sided states we think
we have. Even if a partition function ζ is, in principle, compatible with ER = EPR (for
instance, whenever ζ comes from an actual QFT), we may have artificially or unknowingly
restricted our class X of sources beyond what is necessary to prepare a complete set of
one-sided states.
To illustrate this, note that one obvious way in which ER = EPR might fail is if the
bordism group ΩX
d−1 is non-zero [59, 133], because manifolds B with nonzero bordism class
necessarily have HB = 0. Nevertheless, we will almost always have HB⊔B ̸= 0, as this
two-sided universal Hilbert space contains at least the cylinder states |CB(β)⟩. In order
to restore ER = EPR, we would need to enlarge our source category BordX
d to include
background cobordism defects [59] in order to trivialize the bordism group ΩX
d−1. The only
other way out would be if the cylinder state |CB(β)⟩was null, which happens if and only
if we have ζ(S1
ap × B) = 0. In such a case, the nontrivial bordism class [B] ̸= 0 is not an
obstruction to ER = EPR, because an underlying QFT would not need any states on B.
In such a case, we should say that the corresponding 1-form global symmetry in the bulk
is gauged [59].
We illustrate the obstruction to ER = EPR arising from bordism with the following
example.
Example 5.2. Suppose ζ is the partition function of a spin CFT in d = 2 dimensions,
but we neglect to include any spin fields in our class X of sources. Then, even though
the underlying spin CFT has perfectly factorizing Hilbert spaces, we cannot produce any
states in the Ramond-sector Hilbert space HS1p, because the bordism class [S1
p] ∈ΩSpin
1
is
nonzero. Nevertheless, we could still produce states on S1
p ⊔S1
p, including the Ramond-
sector thermofield double states. Note that including spin fields in our class X of sources
would trivialize the bordism class [S1
p] ∈ΩX
1 , removing the obstruction to ER = EPR.
The bordism group ΩX
d−1 of source manifolds, viewed as semiclassical configurations of
the bulk EFT as in [59, 163], is insufficient to fully capture the breakdown of ER = EPR.
In particular, the semiclassical bordism group is only well-defined in a particular duality
frame, and only directly captures charges associated with the topology of configuration
space. Physically, the issue is that ΩX
d−1 collapses too many configurations into a single
32In other words, ER = EPR holds if the canonical lax monoidal structure on the universal construction
is actually strong monoidal.
– 39 –


## Page 42


equivalence class. To fully capture the breakdown of ER = EPR, we should only identify
two configurations if they are actually equivalent under the gravitational gauge constraints,
not merely if they are connected by some trajectory in the GPI.
The baby universe category CBU, as constructed in Section 7, remedies this issue. While
CBU is graded by ΩX
d−1, the baby universe category remembers the actual quantum state of
the spacetime manifold, and not merely its semiclassical bordism class. We will prove the
following result, showing that CBU is a sharp diagnostic of the breakdown of ER = EPR.
Theorem 5.3. Let ζ be a partition function on a unitary bordism category BordX
d satisfying
Axioms 4.1-4.5. Then ER = EPR holds if and only if the associated baby universe category
is trivial,
CBU = Hilb.
(5.15)
As a result, (5.15) provides a formalization, in the axiomatic framework for quantum
gravity studied in this paper, of the Swampland cobordism conjecture [59] in bulk codimen-
sion two. Theorem 5.3 is a direct analog of the condition HBU = C for partition-function
factorization, which is itself a formalization, in the same framework, of the Swampland
cobordism conjecture in bulk codimension one [26].
When ER = EPR fails, other familiar features of a local QFT will break down as well.
One of these is the relationship between the partition function on a Euclidean circle S1

Gravitationally, the inequality (5.16) has a natural interpretation in the regime where
we expect black holes to dominate the thermal ensemble. The thermal partition function
ζ(S1
β × B) computed by the GPI includes Euclidean black hole configurations in which
the thermal circle shrinks, and gives a result compatible with the Bekenstein–Hawking
entropy. However, trHB(e−β b
