exist as one-sided states preparable by our chosen class of sources. If we have included
sources for all fields in the bulk EFT, then these are precisely the black hole states which
can be formed by the gravitational collapse of EFT degrees of freedom. Morally, one can
view trHB(e−β b
HB) as being computed by a GPI with an additional restriction that the
thermal circle is not allowed to shrink in the bulk (see [110] for further comments).
5.3
Symmetries and missing states
While the universal construction does not define a unitary QFT, let us suppose, temporar-
ily, that there is an underlying QFT with partition function ζ, whose factorizing Hilbert
spaces we denote e
HB. There is a canonical map HB →e
HB from the universal Hilbert
– 40 –


## Page 43


spaces to the Hilbert spaces e
HB, defined by mapping the formal state |M⟩in the universal
Hilbert space to the state |ψM⟩∈e
HB prepared, in the actual QFT, by the path integral
over M in the underlying QFT. In fact, this map defines an isometric embedding HB ⊂e
HB,
since, by construction, the squared norm of the state |M⟩is ζ(M† ◦M), which agrees with
the norm of |ψM⟩by assumption.
Thus, if an underlying QFT exists, the universal construction fails to satisfy ER = EPR
only because it is missing some states. This conclusion fits naturally with the bound (5.16),
which would follow immediately from the presence of an isometric embedding HB ⊂e
HB
compatible with the Hamiltonian (as it would be).
Supposing an underlying QFT does exist, we ask: which states should be missed by
the universal construction? Well, if the underlying QFT has a global symmetry G, and
our class X of sources does not include any fields charged under G, then every state in the
universal construction must be neutral under G. Nevertheless, in a two-boundary Hilbert
space, we may prepare states with non-vanishing one-sided charges, so long as the charges
on the two sides are precisely correlated so that the net charge cancels. For example, the
thermofield double state (5.13) includes fluctuations over all states in the one-sided Hilbert
space, including any one-sided charged states, despite the fact that the net charge of the
thermofield double state is precisely zero.
Thus, in any underlying QFT with a nontrivial global symmetry, ER = EPR cannot
possibly hold unless we include a complete set of charges in our collection X of sources, as
was first observed by Harlow in [41]. Our main result, Theorem 1.1, asserts that: first of
all, an underlying QFT does exist, and is specified uniquely up to unitary isomorphism.
Moreover, the states charged under a global symmetry are the only states which the uni-
versal construction misses, so the clear obstruction described by Harlow [41] is, in fact, the
only obstruction.
The same logic applies to something which is not usually considered a symmetry:
the “symmetry” implemented by the (d −1)-dimensional topological operators defined by
stacking invertible (d −1)-dimensional QFTs on top of the identity operator. In QFT, this
is not usually considered a global symmetry, as it acts on a fixed spatial manifold B by a
theory-independent c-number, entirely determined by the bordism class [B], which can be
removed by a counterterm by definition. Nevertheless, this symmetry obstructs ER = EPR
just the same: it corresponds to the (ΩX
d−1)∨global symmetry described in the Swampland
cobordism conjecture [59],33 obstructing ER = EPR as discussed above.
As a final comment, we note that the obstruction arising from symmetry also means
that any theory which requires fermions cannot possibly satisfy ER = EPR, at least within
the framework we have developed so far. This is because any fermionic QFT has a global ZF
2
symmetry corresponding to fermion parity,34 and as discussed in Section 3.2.1, we cannot
include fermionic sources in a unitary bordism category. We describe how our framework
must be modified to accommodate fermionic sources in Section 10.2.
33This symmetry was independently rediscovered in [164].

6
Examples
In this section, we describe the universally constructed Hilbert spaces in a few simple, low-
dimensional examples. In each example, we study a unitary bordism category equipped
with a partition function ζ satisfying Axioms 4.1-4.5, and directly verify the conclusions of
Theorem 1.1 to the extent that we are able. Our examples are ordered in terms of increasing
complexity, and, correspondingly, decreasing tractability of reproducing Theorem 1.1 by
hand.
6.1
Topological quantum mechanics
Our first example is bosonic topological quantum mechanics (TQM) without time reversal
symmetry. Thus, we take d = 1, and fix a partition function ζ on the unitary bordism
category BordSO
1

we can completely prove Theorem 1.1 by hand. This example is motivated by the GPI of
Marolf and Maxfield [25], and more specifically, by Maxfield’s detailed analysis [73] of the
geometrically preparable states in TQM.
First, by finiteness, reality, and multiplicativity, ζ is specified by the single real number
ζ(S1) = k ∈R. Reflection positivity, applied simply, implies that k ≥0, as the circle is
obtained as the double of a semicircle. The partition function ζ wants to be the partition
function of a topological quantum mechanical theory with k degenerate ground states, but
for this to make sense we would need k to be a non-negative integer.

vanishes if and only if the corresponding Young diagram has more than k rows, and so
states labeled by a Young diagram with more than k rows are null. This generalizes (6.7)
from the antisymmetric tensor representation to an arbitrary representation.

## Page 48


unitary TQFT with EotW branes and with partition function ζ, by choosing kEotW vectors
in Ck with inner product matrix ⟨ψi|ψj⟩. Moreover, any two such choices are unitarily
equivalent, as, up to a unitary change of basis, the Gram matrix is the only invariant of
an ordered list of vectors in a Hilbert space.
Now, let us ask: which new states do the EotW branes add into the universal Hilbert
spaces? Without EotW branes, we saw previously that we could already produce all states
invariant under the U(k) symmetry acting on the k-dimensional space of ground states. Let
us fix an underlying TQFT, corresponding to a fixed choice of kEotW vectors |ψi⟩∈Ck with
inner product matrix (6.13). Let us also choose some orthonormal basis |ϕa⟩of the linear
span of the states |ψi⟩, as above. This orthonormal basis defines an isometric embedding
Cr ,→Ck, which precisely describes the additional states produced by the EotW branes.
Consider the unitary group G = U(k −r) of the orthogonal complement Ck−r ,→Ck
of the space Cr ,→Ck of EotW brane states. We claim that the universal Hilbert spaces of
the theory with EotW branes are precisely the U(k −r)-invariant subspaces of the Hilbert
spaces of the underlying TQFT, on any number of positively and negatively oriented points.
One inclusion is obvious, as we cannot produce any charged states under U(k −r) using
smooth manifolds or EotW branes, by construction.
For the other direction, consider any tensor product of copies of Ck and its conjugate.
We can prepare any state in this tensor product which is given by a tensor product of any
number of vectors in Cr ⊂Ck and any number of invariant tensors under U(k). Decompose
the tensor product as a direct sum of tensor products of Cr, Ck−r, and their conjugates.
We can clearly produce any state in the tensor factors Cr, and we can also prepare any
U(k −r)-invariant state in tensor products of Ck−r and its conjugate by applying Schur–
Weyl duality to the states |PEotW⊥⟩. Thus, we can produce all U(k −r)-invariant states,
as claimed.
This example demonstrates the general manner in which the universal Hilbert spaces
change upon adding additional sources. If the original universal Hilbert spaces were given
by the G-invariants, and we add new sources which break the symmetry to a subgroup H ⊂
G, then we will necessarily produce all the states that are invariant under H (though not
necessarily under G). Again, we see that the only obstruction to states being geometrically
preparable is the presence of a symmetry group.

Instead, we use this example to illustrate an interesting possibility regarding the nature
of the symmetry group G.
In a quantum mechanical system, it is quite easy to find
symmetries, as a symmetry need only commute with the Hamiltonian operator. Thus, the
full unitary symmetry of the Hamiltonian bH discussed above is simply,
G =
Y
n
U(dn),
(6.28)
the product of the unitary groups rotating degenerate states within each energy level. As
topologized in the strong operator topology, G is a compact Hausdorff group, being an
infinite product of Lie groups. However, it is not a Lie group.
Let us consider the states in the universal Hilbert space Hpt+⊔pt−. This is given by the
closed linear span, in H⊗H, of the thermofield double states |TFDβ⟩produced by intervals
of length β/2 as β varies. Certainly, Hpt+⊔pt−is contained in the G-invariant subspace, as
every thermofield double state is G-invariant. Conversely, in each microcanonical window
around an energy level En, there is a unique G-invariant state, which is maximally entangled
within the microcanonical window and pairs each one-sided state with its CPT conjugate.
These states may all be obtained as linear combinations of thermofield double states |TFDβ⟩
by taking an inverse Laplace transform. Thus, the states in the two-boundary universal
Hilbert space Hpt+⊔pt−are precisely the G-invariant states.
As a result, we see that the group G described in Theorem 1.1 can really be a compact
Hausdorff group, and is not always a compact Lie group. That being said, this example
is rather specific to quantum mechanics (d = 1), and the condition for being a symmetry
in higher dimensions is much more restrictive, as a symmetry is required to commute with
the local stress-energy tensor. We do not know any example in higher dimensions where
the group G is not a compact Lie group.
6.5
Two-dimensional Ising CFT
Our final example considered in this section is given by the two-dimensional Ising CFT,
considered as a theory on the geometric bordism category BordRiem,SO
2
of oriented two-
– 49 –


## Page 52


dimensional Riemannian manifolds.36 The Ising CFT has three Virasoro primary operators:
1, σ, and ϵ. It also has a Z2 spin-flip symmetry, under which σ is charged. Under the state-
operator correspondence, every state in the Hilbert space on S1 is a linear combination
of the states corresponding to 1, σ, and ϵ, as well as their Virasoro descendants. We ask:
which of these states are produced by the path integral over a smooth Riemann surface
with one boundary circle? By the state-operator correspondence, this is the same as asking
which operators acquire a one-point function on some closed Riemann surface.
On the sphere, the identity operator 1 certainly acquires a one-point function, as it
corresponds to the vacuum under the state-operator correspondence. However, there are
more operators which acquire a sphere one-point function if we allow an arbitrary metric
on the sphere, or, equivalently, keep the metric fixed but vary the local coordinate sewing
our operator insertion into the sphere. This change of local coordinate is, precisely, the
action of the Virasoro group on the space of local operators. Thus, any state in the vacuum
Virasoro module, corresponding to the stress tensor and its descendants, can be produced
by some possibly-infinite linear combination of appropriately chosen metrics at genus zero.
Moreover, if we parametrize our choice of metric by the choice of Virasoro transformation
used to sew in the local coordinate, the null states in the sense of the universal construction
are, precisely, the null states in the vacuum Virasoro module. By an analogous argument,
it is sufficient, at any genus, to determine which Virasoro primaries acquire a one-point
function.
Let us now move to genus one, where we get additional states.
In particular, the
operator ϵ acquires a torus one-point function [170–172], and so the corresponding state
and all of its descendants are produced by the path integral over a genus one surface
with one circular boundary. More precisely, this path integral will produce some linear
combinations of descendants of 1 and ϵ, and we may subtract off any descendants of 1 by
taking linear combinations with the states already produced at genus zero. Ultimately, at
genus zero and one, we will produce all Virasoro descendants of 1 and ϵ. At this point,
nothing new happens at higher genus, as we have already produced all neutral states under
the Z2 spin flip symmetry.
Now, let us consider the universal construction for the Hilbert space on two circles,
which corresponds to asking about which pairs of operators acquire a non-zero two-point
function on some possibly-disconnected Riemann surface.
Disconnected manifolds will
produce the pairs of operators,
(1, 1),
(ϵ, 1),
(1, ϵ),
(ϵ, ϵ),
(6.29)
and descendants thereof under the two independent copies of Virasoro.
However, we will also get new states from two-point functions on connected Riemann
surfaces. This will include linear combinations of the pairs (6.29) and their descendants,
together with the pair,
(σ, σ),
(6.30)
36We need to choose Riemannian metrics, and not just conformal structures, in order to trivialize the
Weyl anomaly and get a well-defined partition function.
– 50 –


## Page 53


and its descendants, from the non-zero σ two-point function on the sphere. Note that the
pairs (6.29) and (6.30), with their descendants, correspond to all the Z2-neutral states in
the two-circle Hilbert space, consisting of the tensor products of neutral states with neutral
states or charged states with charged states, but not the tensor products of neutral states
with charged states or vice-versa. By similar arguments, we learn that, for any number of
circles, the states in the Ising CFT preparable by manifolds are, precisely, the Z2-neutral
states.
It is worth emphasizing that the Ising CFT has, in addition to the Z2 spin-flip symme-
try, a non-invertible Kramers–Wannier symmetry under which both σ and ϵ are charged.
However, while this non-invertible symmetry is sufficient to prevent ϵ from acquiring a
sphere one-point function, it is insufficient to prevent ϵ from acquiring a torus one-point
function. The non-invertible symmetry is broken by the nontrivial topology of the torus, as
explained in [57, 173]. It is possible that Theorem 1.1 could be used to prove the conjecture
of [57, 173] that every non-invertible symmetry is broken on some topology.37
7
The baby universe category
In this section, we turn to the central object of study in this paper: the baby universe
category CBU associated to an abstract partition function ζ on a unitary bordism category
BordX
d that satisfies axioms 4.1-4.5. Our construction of CBU in this section is, essentially,
a formalization and retelling of the previous work [62, 63] of Colafranceschi, Dong, Marolf,
Zhang, and the second author of this paper.
7.1
Towards a quantum bordism category
The core conceptual idea of our construction of CBU is that, while the universal Hilbert
spaces HB miss some of the states of the putative QFT underlying ζ, these states are
still secretly present as the would-be one sided microstates of ER bridges in HB⊔A, as A
ranges over all possible objects of BordX
d . In general, while ER = EPR may fail, the states
of ER bridges will nevertheless try their best to behave as if they were entangled states.
For instance, the diagonal sector HB⊔B contains the ER bridge prepared by the cylinder
CB(β), which wants to be the thermofield double state at inverse temperature β.
As we have seen in the examples of Section 6, the ER bridge states formally built from
manifolds know a remarkably large amount about the missing states. In fact, example by
example, we found that the ER bridges always seem to know everything about the missing
states, up to the action of a unitary symmetry group acting on the missing states. Our
goal, in this section and the next two, is to systematize the process of distilling information
about the missing states from the collection of ER bridges.
We do this by following a general categorical principle known as Grothendieck’s relative
point of view.
The idea is that, to understand some mathematical object Y in some
37This conjecture does not immediately follow from Theorem 1.1 due to the possibility that some element
of G secretly acts non-invertibly on extended operators, while acting invertibly on the Hilbert space on
any spatial manifold. We find this possibility unlikely, but ruling it out would require us to generalize our
framework to the fully-extended case.
– 51 –


## Page 54


Figure 13. In the universal Hilbert space HB, we only produce states using bordisms ∅→B
(left). Applying Grothendieck’s relative point of view, we should attempt to prepare states on B
from bordisms A →B (center), viewed as recipes for evolving arbitrary states on A into states on
B. Given that we do not have arbitrary states on A, the best we can do is prepare the associated
ER bridge state in HB⊔A (right).
category C, one should consider maps X →Y from every other object X ∈C, rather than
only consider maps 1 →Y from some unit object 1. For instance, if we are doing any sort
of geometry, and 1 denotes a single point, then a map 1 →Y is a point of Y , while a map
X →Y is an X-parametrized family of points of Y . While the bare set of points of Y
does not fully capture its geometry, the knowledge of how its points fit into parametrized
families does. This approach to algebraic geometry is known as the functor of points, and
is justified by the Yoneda Lemma.
Applying Grothendieck’s relative point of view, we learn that we have been making a
mistake by only building states on B from bordisms ∅→B. Instead, we should try to
build states on B from bordisms N : A →B, viewed as recipes for producing states on B
given a reservoir of arbitrary states on A. Given that we do not actually have a reservoir
of arbitrary states on A, the best we can do is prepare the ER bridge state |N⟩in the
Hilbert space HB⊔A. We depict this chain of logic in Figure 13. Our goal now, following
Grothendieck’s relative point of view, is to assemble the Hilbert spaces HB⊔A into some
category in which A and B live as objects.
The most direct approach is to simply take HB⊔A to be the space of morphisms from
A to B. By the fundamental trace inequality of [62] (Proposition B.1), composition of
bordisms induces a jointly continuous bilinear composition map,
HC⊔B × HB⊔A →HC⊔A,
(7.1)
defined by using the unitary structure of BordX
d to compose along the intermediate bound-
ary B, as described in Proposition B.2. The resulting category is typically non-unital, just
like BordX
d , as the cylinder states |CB(β)⟩∈HB⊔B typically do not converge as β →0.
To understand the problem, consider what would happen if we had ER = EPR, so that
the two-boundary Hilbert space did factorize as HA⊔B = HA ⊗HB.
In this case, we
would recognize HA⊔B as the space of Hilbert–Schmidt operators HA →HB, and, as
mentioned in Section 3.2, the identity operator on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space
is never Hilbert–Schmidt. Thus, the universal Hilbert space HB⊔A is a space of what we
might call “Hilbert–Schmidt morphisms” from A to B. We will call the resulting category
the Hilbert–Schmidt baby universe category, and denote it by CHS
BU.
There is nothing wrong with this direct approach, and we could prove all of our main
– 52 –


## Page 55


results by working with CHS
BU alone. However, our main approach in this paper will be
slightly more involved, taking a detour through the world of von Neumann algebras. We
do this for a few reasons: firstly, in order to make more of a direct connection to the moti-
vating previous work [62, 63], and secondly, in order to provide a more easily generalizable
framework. As we will see, the fact that composition of bordisms is jointly continuous in
the Hilbert space topology is a lucky accident, arising physically from the fact that we are
dealing with closed spatial manifolds and, ultimately, atomic (hence in particular Type I)
von Neumann algebras. That being said, our motivating goal is to understand the complete
breakdown of boundary locality in quantum gravity. Any higher-categorical extension of
our framework would require us to cut spatial manifolds open along higher-codimension
entangling surfaces, where we expect to find Type III von Neumann algebras.
To motivate our construction, note that if HB⊔A is the analog of the space of Hilbert–
Schmidt operators between some Hilbert spaces associated to A and B, what we want is
the analog of the space of all bounded operators. These spaces will form the morphisms in
a category CvN
BU, which is a W*-category, the multi-object analog of a von Neumann algebra.
To construct CvN
BU, we will follow [62], and take a double commutant in a natural Hilbert
space representation induced by ζ.

7.3.1
A bit of Tomita–Takesaki theory
Our construction of the von Neumann completion CvN
BU is a natural generalization of a
standard construction in Tomita–Takesaki theory [176, 177].
In this section, we spell
out this analogy, and apply standard techniques from Tomita–Takesaki theory to quickly
reproduce the key analytic results of [62] concerning the structure of the von Neumann
endomorphism algebras AB in CvN
BU.
In Tomita–Takesaki theory, one starts with what is known as a Hilbert algebra: a pre-
Hilbert space Hpre equipped with a (typically non-unital) ∗-algebra structure satisfying a
number of axioms. One then forms the Hilbert space completion H of the pre-Hilbert space
Hpre, and considers the action of Hpre on H induced by left multiplication. The left von
Neumann algebra A of the Hilbert algebra Hpre is the double commutant,
A = (Hpre)′′,
(7.31)
of this collection of left-multiplication operators. By writing the Tomita operator bS : a 7→a†
in terms of its polar decomposition, bS = bJ b∆1/2, one defines the modular conjugation
operator bJ and the operator b∆which implements modular flow. Finally, using modular
conjugation, one proves that H is a standard form representation of the von Neumann
algebra A.
Though not so named, this is precisely the formalism used by [62] to construct von
Neumann algebras AB from the surface algebras Cpre
BU(B →B).42 Above, we have gener-
alized the construction of [62] to the natural multi-object version. The pre-baby universe
category Cpre
BU is an example of what might be called a Hilbert algebroid, namely, a multi-
object version of a Hilbert algebra. By analogy, then, the von Neumann completion CvN
BU
might be called the left von Neumann algebroid of the Hilbert algebroid Cpre
BU. To demystify
the naming conventions, recall that a one-object linear category is entirely determined by
a single algebra, namely the endomorphism algebra of its single object. An algebroid is the
multi-object version of an algebra: a linear category. Thus, “von Neumann algebroid” is
simply a synonym for what we have been calling a W*-category.
The Hilbert algebroid Cpre
BU, and its endomorphism Hilbert algebras [62], are quite
special among all possible Hilbert algebroids and algebras. In a general Hilbert algebra,
one does not require the map a 7→a† to be anti-unitary. If it is, as in the morphism spaces
of Cpre
BU, one obtains what is known as a unimodular Hilbert algebra, which necessarily has
trivial modular flow, b∆= 1. Moreover, in any Hilbert algebra, the formula,
φ(a†a) def
= ⟨a|a⟩,

algebra is unimodular, the weight φ is tracial, φ(a†a) = φ(aa†), and defines an n.s.f. trace
on A. This trace agrees precisely with the trace (7.4) on the unimodular Hilbert algebroid

As motivation, let us recall the gravitational interpretation of the objects in CvN
BU given
by closed (d−1)-dimensional X-manifolds. Given two manifolds A, B ∈CvN
BU, the categorical

possibly including the trivial sort of ER bridge where A and B are not connected. For any
fixed ER bridge, there may be some sort of unsplittable gauge flux flowing along the bridge
from A to B, which may or may not be visible in the EFT description.
Merely fixing the boundary conditions A, B at the boundary of an ER bridge does
not necessarily fully fix the flux flowing through the bridge. Moreover, any fixed flux may
appear with some large multiplicity. We would like to determine the irreducible unsplittable
fluxes which may flow through an ER bridge and obstruct ER = EPR. Thus, our goal now
is to break the objects A, B ∈CvN
BU into their constituent irreducible flux sectors.
We begin by recalling the categorical notion of the image of a projector. If X ∈C is
an object of some category, and P : X →X is a projector, P 2 = P, we say the image of
P, if it exists, is an object im(P) ∈C equipped with inclusion and projection morphisms,
i : im(P) →X,
π : X →im(P),
(7.39)
such that i ◦π = P and π ◦i = idim(P). When an image im(P) exists, it is unique up to
unique isomorphism. In a W*-category, we typically assume our projectors are orthogonal,
P † = P, and demand that the inclusion and projection are related by i† = π. Given an
orthogonal projector P : X →X in a W*-category, we obtain an orthogonal direct sum
decomposition,
X ∼= im(P) ⊕im(1 −P),
(7.40)
whenever both P and 1 −P have images.
However, the existence of images for projectors is far from guaranteed. Let us illustrate
this with an example.

image of a projector X ⊂B.
Taken together, the process of taking the direct-sum completion and the idempotent
completions, in either (equivalent) order, is known as W*-Cauchy completion [75]. For any
W*-category C, we use,

Definition 7.4. The baby universe category is the W*-Cauchy completion,
CBU = Hilb(CvN
BU),
(7.49)
of the von Neumann completion CvN
BU of the pre-baby universe category Cpre
BU.
As mentioned above, the process of W*-Cauchy completion, and in particular the
idempotent completion, is a categorical analog of the Cauchy completion of a pre-Hilbert
space. The categorical analog of the quotient by null states is the following. While the
objects of Cpre
BU have a simple, geometric description as closed (d−1)-dimensional manifolds,
the objects in CBU have no such simple description. In particular, while every object of
CBU may be written as a categorical superposition of images of projectors on manifolds,
this expression need not be unique. This is because there may be nontrivial isomorphisms
between images of different projectors acting on different manifolds. Before taking the
idempotent completion, the objects B ∈Cpre
BU are linearly independent, but once we pass to
CBU and expand them in terms of their component pieces, we may learn that they satisfy
categorical linear relations.
Gravitationally, let us recall from Section 5.1 that Hpre
B
is a vector space of kinematic
states, while HB is a Hilbert space of physical states arising from projecting onto the
– 64 –


## Page 67


solutions to the Hamiltonian constraint equation associated to bulk time evolution. Analo-
gously, Cpre
BU is a category of kinematic objects, while CBU is a category of physical objects.45
We view the nontrivial isomorphisms in CBU as a categorical version of the Hamiltonian
constraint, arising from the fact that the categorical evolution of a codimension-two slice
of a bulk spacetime along codimension-one ER bridges is pure gauge.
Let us describe another way of seeing that passing to the W*-Cauchy completion
includes a categorical analog of taking the quotient by null states. First, note that two states
|ψ1⟩, |ψ2⟩are equivalent modulo null states if and only if they have the same wavefunction
overlap with all states,
|ψ1⟩∼|ψ2⟩⇐⇒⟨ϕ|ψ1⟩= ⟨ϕ|ψ2⟩, ∀|ϕ⟩.
(7.50)
While the null state |ψ2⟩−|ψ1⟩has no categorical analog, as we cannot subtract objects
in a category, we do have an analog of (7.50). Let Ψ1, Ψ2 ∈CBU be any objects. Then we
have,
Ψ1 ∼= Ψ2 ⇐⇒⟨⟨Φ|Ψ1⟩⟩∼= ⟨⟨Φ|Ψ2⟩⟩, ∀Φ ∈CBU.
(7.51)
In fact, (7.51) does not merely describe a logical implication, but an isomorphism between
the space of unitary isomorphisms Ψ1 ∼= Ψ2 in CBU and the space of unitary natural
isomorphisms ⟨⟨−|Ψ1⟩⟩∼= ⟨⟨−|Ψ2⟩⟩between the functors ⟨⟨−|Ψi⟩⟩: CBU →Hilb defined by
taking a categorical wavefunction overlap.
More formally, (7.51) is the W*-categorical Yoneda Lemma [75, Prop. 4.12], which
asserts that the W*-categorical Yoneda embedding,
C →FunW*(C, Hilb),
Ψ 7→⟨⟨−|Ψ⟩⟩,
(7.52)
is fully faithful, for any W*-category C. Moreover, the second part of [75, Prop. 4.12]
asserts further that (7.52) presents FunW*(C, Hilb) as the W*-Cauchy completion Hilb(C).
Thus, in the sense of (7.51), passing from C to Hilb(C) involves a categorical quotient by
null states.
7.4.1
Decomposition into µ-sectors
Our goal, now, is to describe the structure of the Cauchy complete W*-category CBU.
This structure is entirely determined by the collection of projectors in the von Neumann
endomorphism algebras AB, as studied in [62].
As we saw in Section 7.3.1, the trace
inequality implies that the von Neumann algebras AB are atomic, so that every projector
is an orthogonal direct sum of minimal projectors. This translates into a sort of infinitary
semisimplicity for CBU: every object X ∈CBU, including those associated to manifolds B,
decomposes as a possibly-infinite orthogonal direct sum of simple objects.46
45The objects of CBU, given by categorical quantum states of bulk-codimension two slices, may be viewed

varieties to the category of pure Chow motives is also given by linearization and idempotent completion
(see, e.g., [180]).
46Equivalently, we have a canonical equivalence CBU = Rep(Z(CBU)), where the commutative atomic von

7.4.2
Finite-dimensional objects
We have seen that CBU, being a Cauchy complete atomic W*-category, is an infinitary
version of a semisimple category. For some purposes, and particularly in order to eventually
apply the DR reconstruction theorem, it will be helpful to work with an actual semisimple
category. Thus, let us define Cf.d.
BU to be the full sub-category of CBU containing all finite
direct sums of µ-sectors. Cf.d.
BU is semisimple by construction. Importantly, the full baby
universe category may be easily recovered from Cf.d.
BU by “extension of scalars” from Vec to
Hilb. More formally, CBU is recovered from Cf.d.
BU by W*-Cauchy completion,
CBU = Hilb(Cf.d.
BU),
(7.61)
which corresponds to allowing infinite direct sums when it previously only had finite ones.
Thus, the structure of CBU is entirely determined by Cf.d.
BU, even though the objects B ∈
CBU prepared by manifolds will typically not be finite-dimensional (outside of topological
theories).
We may obtain an important alternate characterization of Cf.d.
BU through the use of the
canonical trace on CBU. For any object X ∈CBU, we define its categorical dimension by,
dX = dim(X) def
= tr(idX),
(7.62)
and we say that X is finite-dimensional if it has finite categorical dimension. By positivity
of the trace, we have dX ≥0 for all X ∈CBU, and in fact, we even have dX ≥1 for any
nonzero object X, by the lower bound (7.35) on the trace of any projector. As the trace on
CBU is semifinite, each µ-sector is necessarily finite-dimensional, as otherwise we could find
a smaller, finite-dimensional sub-object of µ. As a result, an object is finite dimensional if
and only if it is a finite direct sum of µ-sectors, and so Cf.d.
BU is precisely the full subcategory
of CBU on the finite-dimensional objects.

d , we now discuss the
canonical symmetric monoidal structure on CBU induced by the disjoint union of manifolds.

8.2
Duals and rigidity
While the non-unital categories BordX
d , Cpre
BU, and even CHS
BU are non-unital rigid monoidal
categories (which we recall means that every object has a dual), the von Neumann com-
– 70 –


## Page 73


pletion CvN
BU and the baby universe category CBU itself are not. This is because, in forming
the von Neumann completion, we have introduced weak operator limits of morphisms
Oi : B1 →B2 which may not converge when considered as states |Oi⟩. These morphisms
include, in particular, the identity morphisms for any infinite-dimensional object.
However, some objects X ∈CBU do admit duals, which are given by the conjugates
X when they exist.
In fact, an object in X ∈CBU admits a dual if and only if it is
finite-dimensional. This is because an object X ∈CBU has a dual if and only if idX is
Hilbert–Schmidt, if and only if the limit as β →0 of the cylinder states |CX(β)⟩exists, if
and only if we may use that limit to define evaluation and coevaluation maps,
evX : X ⊔X →∅,
coevX : ∅→X ⊔X,
(8.9)
forming a standard solution to the conjugate equations.48
Thus, we see that the full subcategory Cf.d.
BU ⊂CBU on the finite-dimensional objects
is also exactly the full subcategory on the dualizable objects. This immediately implies
that Cf.d.
BU is closed under disjoint union, as the fusion of dualizable objects in any monoidal
category is always dualizable (with dual the fusion of the duals). Thus, Cf.d.
BU becomes a
symmetric monoidal category in its own right. In fact, we may see this more directly: the
trace on CBU is monoidal, in that we have,
tr(O1 ⊔O2) = tr(O1)tr(O2).
(8.10)
Thus, the categorical dimension is multiplicative under disjoint union, and so the disjoint
union of finite-dimensional objects must be finite-dimensional.
As a result, Cf.d.
BU is a rigid, symmetric monoidal, idempotent complete C*-category
with simple tensor unit, otherwise known as a unitary tensor category. Moreover, many
of the structures we have discussed reduce to canonical structures on any unitary tensor
category, including the canonical balanced unitary dual functor (UDF) X 7→X [127], the
induced positive trace, and the twist automorphism θX.

9
Reconstruction
In this section, we conclude our proof of Theorem 1.1, by applying the Doplicher–Roberts
(DR) reconstruction theorem [76, 77] to the baby universe category.
9.1
Rigidly generated W*-tensor categories
Before we begin introducing the DR reconstruction theorem, we pause to introduce the
following notion. We do this because the DR reconstruction theorem, as usually stated,
does not directly apply to CBU, as discussed further below.
Definition 9.1. Let C be a W*-tensor category, which we recall means a W*-tensor cat-
egory in the sense of [75] which is additionally Cauchy complete and whose tensor unit is
simple. We say that C is rigidly generated if it is generated by some collection of dualizable
objects. We say the same for a W*-multitensor category, whose unit may not be simple.
By [181], we do not need to separately assume that a rigidly generated W*-tensor
category is atomic, as the irreducibility of the unit implies that every dualizable object is a
finite direct sum of simple objects. Conversely, every finite direct sum of simple objects is
dualizable, as every simple object is a subobject of a dualizable object by rigid generation.
– 73 –


## Page 76


As we have seen, CBU is rigidly generated, as it is generated by the subcategory Cf.d.
BU of
dualizable objects.
In order to understand the general structure of rigidly generated W*-tensor categories,
let UTC denote the (2, 1)-category of unitary tensor categories, unitary monoidal functors,
and unitary monoidal natural transformations, and let W∗TCr.g. denote the (2, 1)-category
of rigidly generated W*-tensor categories, W*-tensor functors, and unitary monoidal nat-
ural transformations. Consider the 2-functors,
UTC
W∗TCr.g.
Hilb(−)
(−)f.d.
(9.1)
given respectively, by taking the W*-Cauchy completion and restricting to the full sub-
category of dualizable objects. The second 2-functor (−)f.d. is well defined, because any
monoidal functor takes dualizable objects to dualizable objects, and the tensor product of
two dualizable objects is always dualizable.
These 2-functors (9.1) form an adjoint equivalence of (2, 1)-categories, and so from an
external perspective, there is really no difference at all between unitary tensor categories
and rigidly generated W*-tensor categories. As a result, we may transfer any categorical
properties whatsoever between UTC and W∗TCr.g., as well as their analogous symmetric
monoidal versions. For instance, by equipping every symmetric unitary tensor category
with its canonical balanced UDF, we learn that any rigidly generated symmetric W*-
tensor category is equipped with a canonical bi-involutive structure, which restricts to this
balanced UDF on the subcategory of dualizable objects.
More significantly, we can directly transfer the DR reconstruction theorem, which is
usually stated as a theorem about unitary tensor categories, to a result about rigidly gen-
erated symmetric W*-tensor categories. We could have skipped this section, and neglected
to present Definition 9.1, by applying the DR reconstruction theorem to the unitary tensor
category Cpre
BU, and then repeatedly translating these results by hand to CBU itself. Indeed,
from the perspective of the fusion of µ-sectors, CBU itself is auxiliary. However, CBU plays
a very important role for us, as it admits a canonical functor BordX
d →CBU, embedding
the geometric manifolds of BordX
d into the linear category CBU. As discussed above, this
map does not typically land in the unitary tensor category Cf.d.
BU, since geometric manifolds
require infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces in most geometric QFTs.
A second reason to work with rigidly generated W*-tensor categories, as opposed to
unitary tensor categories, is that there is no analogous adjoint equivalence once we drop the
simplicity of the tensor unit, hence passing to rigidly generated W*-multitensor categories.
This is because the results of [181] no longer apply in the multitensor setting, and a
rigidly generated W*-multitensor category need not be atomic.
For instance, consider
the W*-multitensor category of measurable fields of Hilbert spaces over the interval [0, 1],
equipped with the fiberwise tensor product. The tensor unit is the constant field C, which
is a dualizable generating object all on its own. We discuss this generalization in Section
– 74 –


## Page 77


10.3, in the context of a diffuse space of α-sectors.
9.2
Statistics and fiber functors
We now recall some preliminary notions which enter in the statement of the DR reconstruc-
tion theorem. First, given a simple object µ in a rigidly generated symmetric W*-tensor
category C, consider the twist automorphism θµ : µ →µ defined by the canonical bi-
involutive structure. As (−1)F squares to one, θµ may only take the values ±1, since µ
is simple.
We say that µ is a boson (respectively a fermion) if θµ = +1 (respectively
θµ = −1), More generally, an object X is bosonic (respectively fermionic) if it is a direct
sum of bosons (respectively fermions) only, and define the fermion parity FX of X to be
(+1) (respectively (−1)), so that we have θB = (−1)FX for homogeneous objects X. We
say that the category C is bosonic if every simple object in C is a boson (so that θX = idX
for all X), and otherwise that C is fermionic.
The central objects of interest in DR reconstruction, in the bosonic case, are fiber
functors, namely, symmetric W*-tensor functors
Z : C →Hilb,
(9.2)
from a bosonic symmetric W*-tensor category C to Hilb. In the fermionic case, we must
instead consider super-fiber functors,
Z : C →sHilb,
(9.3)
to the category of super-Hilbert spaces. By the categorical spin-statistics theorem, a super-
fiber functor must take bosons to bosons and fermions to fermions, and thus a fiber functor
on C will land in the subcategory Hilb ⊂sHilb if and only if C is bosonic. Thus, nothing
is lost if we always assume that the target of our fiber functors is sHilb, and we will drop
the prefix “super,” calling either (9.2) or (9.3) a fiber functor.
9.2.1
Fiber functors as QFTs
What would a fiber functor on the baby universe category CBU give us? By pre-composing
with the canonical unitary symmetric monoidal functor BordX
d →CBU, we would obtain a
unitary symmetric monoidal functor,
Z : BordX
d →sHilb,
(9.4)
or in other words, a unitary functorial QFT! The condition of non-degeneracy for this
QFT follows from the non-degeneracy of the functor BordX
d →CBU itself (Proposition
B.11). Moreover, the partition function of any QFT produced this way will automatically
be ζ, as a closed X-manifold M maps to multiplication by the complex number ζ(M)
already in CBU.
In fact, the converse is true as well: any unitary QFT on BordX
d with partition function
ζ automatically extends uniquely to a fiber functor Z : CBU →sHilb. We prove this formally
in Appendix C, but at a heuristic level, note that the action of Z on manifolds is entirely

What we prove in Appendix C is even stronger: the same conclusion holds even if we re-

9.3
Doplicher–Roberts reconstruction
We now complete our proof of Theorem 1.1 by recalling the DR reconstruction theorem,
in three parts: Theorems 9.2, 9.4, and 9.6. We will refer to these as parts (a), (b), and (c),
as they mirror parts (a), (b), and (c) of Theorem 1.1.
As discussed above, the version of the DR reconstruction theorem proven in [76, 77]
applies to a symmetric unitary tensor category, such as Cf.d.. However, we will instead
describe DR reconstruction as a theorem about rigidly generated symmetric W*-tensor
categories, by transfer along the adjoint equivalence (9.1).
9.3.1
Existence of fiber functors
The first part of the DR reconstruction theorem states the following:
Theorem 9.2 (Doplicher–Roberts, part (a)). Let C be a rigidly generated symmetric W*-
tensor category. Then there exists a fiber functor Z : C →sHilb.
Applying Theorem 9.2 to the rigidly generated baby universe category CBU, we obtain
a fiber functor CBU →sHilb. Using the equivalence between fiber functors on CBU and
unitary QFTs with partition function ζ (Proposition C.2), we prove part (a) of Theorem
1.1:
Theorem 9.3 (Theorem 1.1, part (a)). Let ζ be a partition function on a unitary bordism
category BordX
d satisfying Axioms 4.1-4.5. Then there exists a unitary QFT with partition
function ζ.
– 76 –

Let us outline the proof of Theorem 9.2, as a good deal of it is physically illuminating.
In fact, much of the proof has, remarkably, recently reappeared in the context of the
GPI [25, 62, 63], at least in the bosonic case. The most important step is to prove that
the categorical dimension dX of any finite-dimensional object X ∈C is a non-negative
integer, dX ∈Z≥0. This is necessary for any fiber functor to exist: fiber functors preserve
categorical dimensions, and the dimensions of finite-dimensional objects in sHilb are non-
negative integers.49
The standard proof that dX ∈Z≥0 is a simple generalization of the anti-symmetrization
argument we saw in Section 6.1.

quantum gravity needed in order to trivialize the bordism class [µ].

9.3.2
Uniqueness of fiber functors
We have seen that, by Theorem 9.2, any rigidly generated symmetric W*-tensor category
admits a fiber functor.
The second part of the DR reconstruction theorem states the
following:
Theorem 9.4 (Doplicher–Roberts, part (b)). Let C be a rigidly generated symmetric W*-
tensor category. Then any two unitary fiber functors Z1, Z2 : C →sHilb are unitarily
isomorphic.
Applying Theorem 9.4 to CBU, we prove part (b) of Theorem 1.1, stating that uni-
tary QFTs are uniquely determined, up to unitary isomorphism, by their closed manifold
partition functions:
Theorem 9.5 (Theorem 1.1, part (b)). Any two unitary QFTs BordX
d →sHilb which
share the same partition function ζ on all closed X-manifolds are unitarily isomorphic.

9.3.3
The symmetry group
The final part of the DR reconstruction theorem concerns, not the uniqueness of the fiber
functor Z itself, but instead the uniqueness of the unitary isomorphism g : Z1 ⇒Z2
between any two fiber functors. To discuss this uniqueness, let us consider the groupoid
Spec(C), whose objects are fiber functors C →sHilb, and whose morphisms are unitary
monoidal natural isomorphisms. We use the notation Spec(C) for this groupoid, as it is
the categorical analog of the spectrum of a commutative C*-algebra (see Section 9.4 and
[182] for more on this perspective).
Theorem 9.2 states that Spec(C) is nonempty, and Theorem 9.4 states that Spec(C) is
connected. Thus, Spec(C) is characterized (up to equivalence) by its fundamental group at
some basepoint Z ∈Spec(C), defined to be the group
G def
= Aut⊗,†(Z),
(9.13)
of unitary monoidal natural automorphisms g : Z ⇒Z of the chosen fiber functor. Ex-
panding g in components as in (9.12) defines an embedding G ⊂Q
µ U(dµ) as a closed
subgroup of a product of unitary groups, and thus G is a compact Hausdorff group.51 If G
is nontrivial, then while any two fiber functors are unitarily isomorphic, they will not be
uniquely unitarily isomorphic, which is why we still speak of “choosing” a fiber functor.
A useful point of comparison is the example of topological quantum mechanics, as
considered in Section 6.1, with ζ(S1) = k, whose Hilbert space on a point is some k-
dimensional Hilbert space. Of course, every k-dimensional Hilbert space is unitarily equiv-
alent to Ck.
However, there is no canonical choice for this unitary equivalence, as an
abstract k-dimensional Hilbert space does not come with a canonical orthonormal basis.
This fact is absolutely essential to many quantum-mechanical phenomena, including but
not limited to: degenerate perturbation theory, Berry connections, anomalies, and the
very possibility of Yang–Mills fields. As we will see, the U(k) ambiguity of choosing a
k-dimensional Hilbert space is precisely the ambiguity in choosing a fiber functor for the
baby universe category CBU associated to this example.
In general, the automorphism group G of a fiber functor Z : CBU →sHilb has a
natural interpretation in terms of the associated unitary QFT: it is merely a faithfully-
acting unitary symmetry group, in the usual sense. The component of a unitary monoidal
51G is a Lie group if and only if C admits a finite-dimensional tensor generator.

We may now state the final part of the DR reconstruction theorem, which, with a fiber
functor given, is the concrete reconstruction theorem of Tannaka [183] and Krein [184].
Theorem 9.6 (Doplicher–Roberts, part (c)). Let C be a rigidly generated symmetric W*-
tensor category, and let Z : C →sHilb be a fiber functor (whose existence is guaranteed
by Theorem 9.2). Then the canonical lift ZG : C →sRep(G) described in (9.18) is an
equivalence of symmetric W*-tensor categories.
Applied to a fiber functor on CBU, we obtain a complete characterization of the µ-
sectors: they are precisely the irreducible unitary super-representations of a symmetry G
of the associated unitary QFT. As the µ-sectors characterize the breakdown of ER = EPR,
we learn that the only obstruction to ER = EPR, in any theory of quantum gravity, is the
incompleteness of the spectrum of charged states under a symmetry group G. Moreover,
the identification of CBU with sRep(G) allows us to more explicitly identify the multi-
boundary universal Hilbert spaces. Finally, by computing the right-hand side of (8.16) in
sRep(G), we prove part (c) of Theorem 1.1.
Theorem 9.7 (Theorem 1.1, part (c)). Fix a unitary QFT on BordX

As a result, we may reinterpret any breakdown of ER = EPR as the result of projecting
onto the neutral sector of an actual unitary QFT.

To see why this is the case, recall that the stack BG consists of a single point with
automorphism group G. To specify a family over BG, one simply specifies the fiber X over
the single point, and then specifies transition data encoding how X comes back to itself
after traveling along an automorphism. This transition data, consisting of a compatible
family of automorphisms of X for each g ∈G, is the same thing as an action of G on X.
Further, in complete generality, the space of global sections over BG is the space XG of
homotopy fixed points. This is because a global section is a choice of element x ∈X (the
value of the section at the single point) which glues to itself under the transition functions,
meaning g · x = x for all g ∈G.
As a result, a Hilbert space H with a unitary G-representation is simply a field of

We now illustrate our entire construction in three examples, where we can rely on known

Composition is defined formally by convolving the induced partitions, with any handles

This description of CBU for the two-dimensional TQFT with ζ(Σg) = k is, precisely,
the formal category Rep(St) constructed by Deligne in [186], again up to a translation from
unitary to algebraic representation theory. The number k of vacua in the TQFT plays the
role of the formal variable t, and moreover, when t = k ∈Z≥0 we simply obtain, precisely,
CBU = Rep(Sk), after quotienting by null states. Under this identification, S1 ∈CBU maps
to the defining permutation representation Ck of Sk, and the hemisphere ∅→S1 maps to
the inclusion of the trivial representation of Sk into Ck as the vector (1, . . . , 1) (of squared
norm k, compatibly with ζ(S2) = k).
Our formalism shows, then, that the universal construction for this two-dimensional
TQFT produces, precisely, the set of states which are neutral under the Sk symmetry
permuting the vacua. Note that CBU is smart enough to know the difference between TQM
and two-dimensional TQFT: while the total symmetry of a space of k degenerate ground
states is U(k), the only valid local symmetry of the two-dimensional TQFT is Sk, due to
the superselection into vacua.
10
Robustness and extensions
We have completed our construction of CBU and proof of Theorem 1.1. Now, in this section,
we discuss the robustness of our framework as well as some simple extensions. First, we
discuss the extent to which CBU depends on the choice of counterterms, and how it behaves
under stacking of theories. We then describe how fermionic sources require us to generalize
our framework, as is necessary to restore ER = EPR in any fermionic theory. Finally, we
sketch how our framework might be generalized once Axiom 4.3 is dropped, so that we
have a non-factorizing partition function and are forced to deal with α-sectors.
10.1
Counterterm independence and stacking
From the perspective of formal high-energy theory, a natural concern is that our construc-
tion requires us to know the closed-manifold partition function ζ as a fixed finite number.
As discussed in Section 4.2, continuum QFTs are frequently considered only up to equiva-
lence under stacking of counterterms, meaning invertible unitary QFTs BordX
d →sHilb×.

partition function. As invertible theories are, by definition, theories which are invertible
under stacking, we make the following definition.

counterterms.
10.2
Including fermionic sources
We now remedy a major flaw of the framework developed in the bulk of this paper, which
is that it cannot accommodate fermionic sources. As discussed in Section 3.2.2, the issue
is not unitarity, but symmetric monoidality. We illustrate this issue with the following
example, continuing from Sections 6.3 and 9.5.2.
Example 10.2. Consider the partition function on BordSpin
1
given by,
ζ(S1
ap) = +1,
ζ(S1
p) = −1.
(10.12)
As we have seen, this example has an underlying fermionic TQM with a single fermionic
state on pt+, and has baby universe category CBU = sRep(U(1)spinc). Suppose we wanted to
adjoin an end-of-the-world-brane to break the U(1)spinc global symmetry, as in Section 6.2,
or as in our review of the proof of the DR reconstruction theorem. This would correspond
to adjoining some new bordism ψ : ∅→pt+, which could be viewed as an interval with a
free endpoint marked by ψ. No matter how hard we work, any geometric bordism ψ will
satisfy,
θpt+ ◦ψ = ψ,
spt+⊔pt+ ◦(ψ ⊔ψ) = ψ ⊔ψ,
(10.13)
since the twist and symmetry are monoidal natural transformations, which necessarily
trivialize on ∅.

Consider the three states |idpt+⟩, |θpt+⟩, |ψ ⊔ψ†⟩∈Hpt+⊔pt−. By reflection positivity,
we must have ⟨ψ|ψ⟩≥0, and unless it vanishes we can rescale to assume |ψ⟩is normalized.

This is not positive semidefinite, and the only way out would be for ψ to be a null morphism.
The issue is that we tried to adjoin a necessarily-bosonic state to a theory which needed a
fermionic one.
As demonstrated by this example, the issue arises from our requirement that the twist
and symmetry be symmetric monoidal. The resolution is physically obvious: we should
make fermionic sources anti-commute at spacelike separation, and transform by a sign when
acted on by the twist. However, in the non-linear bordism category BordX
d , there is no
way to parse “anti-commute” or “transform by a sign,” as we cannot multiply a bordism
by (−1).
The resolution, to both issues, is that we need to start in a linear category. This is
harmless for the previously considered case without fermionic sources, as our construction
proceeds through the free linearization Cpre
BU = C[BordX
d ] anyway. Starting with a linear
category is also necessary if we already have some linear structure on sources, as it allows
us to parse the constraint that ζ should depend linearly on sources. For example, if we
start with some vector space of local operators, and we want X to include points marked
by operator insertions, we would need to pick a basis of operators if we wanted to define ζ
on a non-linear bordism category.
Thus, let us assume we are given a linear category Cpre
BU, which we formally think of as
some linearization of a unitary bordism category. We need to modify a few of the definitions
of a unitary structure, such as requiring the conjugation and †-structure,

this sense, being a ∗-algebra homomorphism. One could even go so far as to say that the
CPT theorem in QFT is the statement that CPT must be gauged in holographic quantum
gravity.
Just as [65] argued that CPT should be gauged in quantum gravity, we argue that
fermion parity must also be gauged, in the sense that physically-reasonable QFTs (i.e.,
fiber functors) CBU →sHilb should intertwine the bosonic structures. By the spin-statistics
theorem, any fiber functor CBU →sHilb must intertwine the bosonic structures in this way.
So, in some sense, we could say that the spin-statistics theorem is the statement that
fermion parity must be gauged in holographic quantum gravity.

d →CBU as a relative QFT, in the sense of [119], whose bulk is given by flat
G gauge theory in any higher number of dimensions, which has a dual Rep(G) symmetry.
Picking a fiber functor corresponds to picking a gapped Dirichlet boundary condition for
this bulk, which picks a global form of BordX
d →CBU and turns it into an absolute QFT.
This perspective fits well with the observations of [190–192]. More generally, when there are
multiple non-isomorphic α-sectors, arising from a non-factorizing partition function, the
bulk of this relative QFT will decompose into a direct sum over multiple vacua, matching
the discussion in [193, 194] about the relationship between ensembles and decomposition.
11
Discussion
In this paper, we have studied the issue of Hilbert-space factorization in quantum gravity.
We have worked within the axiomatic framework, described in Sections 2-5, in which we
assume we have been given a factorizing, reflection-positive partition function ζ defined on
some class X of source manifolds which serve as the formal boundaries of a putative GPI.
By providing a proof of Theorem 1.1, we have learned that, within this framework, any
breakdown of Hilbert-space factorization is a red herring: there is always an underlying
unitary QFT with partition function ζ, and the Hilbert spaces of gravitational states are
precisely the invariant subsector of this underlying QFT with respect to a global symmetry
group G. Thus, we have learned that the necessary condition of Harlow [41] for ER = EPR
is, in fact, also sufficient.
11.1
Baby universe field theory and averaged holography
Given our results, there are now two perspectives one could take on a GPI which fails to
satisfy ER = EPR, which parallel the perspectives called baby universe field theory and
averaged holography in [32] on a GPI which produces non-factorizing partition functions.
To describe the corresponding perspective on non-factorizing Hilbert spaces, let us first
review these perspectives.
• Baby universe field theory: The baby universe Hilbert space HBU is genuinely
nontrivial, and multi-boundary partition functions do not factorize. The different
α-states are actual distinct states in a theory of quantum gravity. Information which
falls into a black hole ends up in a baby universe, and does not come out in the
Hawking radiation. Quantum gravity can have global 0-form symmetries.
• Averaged holography: The baby universe Hilbert space HBU is an auxiliary tool,
and we are secretly studying a classical ensemble, parametrized by the α-sectors,
of different boundary conditions for the GPI. Each member of this ensemble has a
– 97 –


## Page 100


trivial baby universe Hilbert space and holographically factorizing partition function.
In each member, black holes evaporate unitarily. Quantum gravity has no global 0-
form symmetries.
The result of Coleman, Giddings, Strominger, Marolf, and Maxfield [19–21, 25] justifies
the perspective of averaged holography by showing that any baby universe field theory is,
secretly, nothing but the ensemble average of underlying holographic partition functions.
In this paper, we have provided a justification for the analogous perspective, which we call
higher averaged holography, on a GPI with non-factorizing Hilbert spaces. Higher averaged
holography stands in contrast to what might be called extended baby universe field theory.
These perspectives are given as follows.
• Extended baby universe field theory: The baby universe category CBU is gen-
uinely nontrivial in quantum gravity. The universal Hilbert spaces HB are the actual
multi-boundary Hilbert spaces, and do not factorize. The Bekenstein–Hawking for-
mula is incorrect.56
Quantum gravity can have global 1-form symmetries and an
incomplete spectrum of charged states. ER ̸= EPR.
• Higher averaged holography: The baby universe category CBU is an auxiliary
tool, and we are secretly studying a higher ensemble average of underlying holo-
graphically dual QFTs. The missing one-sided black hole microstates are present
in the underlying UV-complete theory, which has a trivial baby universe category.
The Bekenstein–Hawking formula is correct. Quantum gravity has no global 1-form
symmetries and a complete spectrum of charged states. ER = EPR.
While our expectation is that a UV-complete theory of quantum gravity is holographic,
let us note that baby universe field theories (BUFTs) may still prove to be objects of
mathematical interest. It would be interesting to establish a full set of axioms for functorial
BUFT; our expectation is that they would involve an appropriately lax form of symmetric
monoidal functors BordX
d →sHilb. Further, we expect BUFTs, in general, to sit somewhere
in between the universal construction and the underlying QFT, potentially having more
states than the universal construction but fewer than the full holographic QFT. In fact, we
expect BUFTs on BordX
d with partition function ζ to have a sort of Galois theory, being
classified by the subgroup H ⊂G under which all of their states are neutral.57
11.2
Comparison with past literature
We now compare our results with previous studies of Hilbert-space factorization in the
literature.
First, our conclusion fits well with the previously-observed pattern [43–51]
that gravitational Hilbert spaces seem to automatically factorize once enough degrees of
freedom are included. Moreover, it fits especially well with the observations of [73] where
56In the sense that the actual entropy of black hole microstates, derived from actual states in the one-sided
Hilbert spaces, does not satisfy the Bekenstein–Hawking formula, and is instead smaller (as is necessarily
the case, given Proposition B.12).
57If such a Galois theory exists, then the universal construction plays the role of the ground field, while
the underlying holographic QFT plays the role of the algebraic closure.
– 98 –


## Page 101


our exact conclusions were demonstrated in a toy model (reviewed in Section 6.1). It also,
of course, fits well with the previous work [62, 63] which served as the technical backbone
of this paper. This paper completes the analysis of [62, 63] by studying the case with more
than two boundaries, and explaining that the µ-sectors are nothing but the irreducible
representations of the symmetry group G.
Let us emphasize an important distinction regarding the proper meaning of Hilbert-
space factorization.
Holography requires, not just that multi-boundary Hilbert spaces
happen to factorize as some tensor product (indeed, this requirement is vacuous), but that
the canonical map,
⊔: HB1 ⊗HB2 →HB1⊔B2,
|M1⟩⊗|M2⟩7→|M1 ⊔M2⟩,
(11.1)
is an isomorphism. This distinction is relevant, for example, in the case of JT gravity
with matter (as studied in, e.g., [46]), where the two-boundary Hilbert space factorizes,
but the map (11.1) cannot possibly be an isomorphism. This is because, in the absence
of end-of-the-world (EotW) branes, the one-boundary Hilbert space vanishes, due to the
obstruction arising from bordism.
What seems to be happening is that, in JT gravity with matter, the only remaining
obstruction is the one arising from bordism, so that the object pt+ ∈CBU is invertible
under disjoint union (with inverse pt−).
As with any invertible object, we must have
dpt+ = 1, and so it only takes a single species of EotW brane to get the map (11.1) to be
an isomorphism. Thus, every two-sided state in JT gravity with matter can be viewed as
arising from gluing this unique EotW brane to its conjugate, with any profile of matter
excitations coming along for the ride. The invertibility of pt+ ∈CBU under disjoint union
makes this gluing invisible, which is why it played no role in the analysis of [46].
Our analysis also correctly matches the known reason why the two-sided Hilbert space
of pure JT gravity, without matter, does not factorize in any meaningful sense. Putting
aside issues with drawing from the ensemble of [1], whatever the α-sector theories may be,
they must be some quantum mechanical systems. As we saw in Example 6.4, quantum
mechanical systems always have, at least, the symmetry group which rotates each energy
level independently. Gravitational states in pure JT gravity only produce neutral states,
or in other words, states with equal energy as measured on the two sides; this is merely
the gravitational gauge constraint of JT gravity. This constraint is lifted in the presence
of matter, which allows us to insert charged operators as additional sources (compare with
[43, 44, 195]). This breaks the corresponding symmetry to the diagonal U(1), which is
associated with the bordism group ΩSO
1
= Z and can only be broken by EotW branes.
11.3
Bulk interpretation of the symmetry group
The symmetry group G appearing in Theorem 1.1 has, so far, only been discussed as a global
symmetry of the holographically dual QFT associated to a bulk theory of quantum gravity.
We now comment on the bulk interpretation. At first glance, G should be interpreted as
a bulk gauge group without any charged states, in line with the motivating example of
Einstein–Maxwell theory [41]. In some sense, this interpretation is correct, as every global
– 99 –


## Page 102


symmetry of the holographically dual QFT is usually viewed as the dual of a bulk gauge
symmetry under the holographic dictionary.
However, putting aside our specific discussion, the matching between a boundary global
symmetry and a bulk gauge symmetry requires caveats. First of all, it does not correctly
type check: a boundary global symmetry is a duality invariant notion, while a bulk gauge
symmetry is not invariant under duality. The resolution is that the correct bulk dual of
a boundary global symmetry is a boundary-localized global symmetry of the theory of
quantum gravity, as defined with the boundary held fixed as a source. Such boundary-
localized symmetries arise when the bulk has a deconfined gauge field with a Dirichlet
boundary condition, but may arise in many other ways.
In the context of AdS/CFT,
having a boundary-localized global symmetry is the same as having a long-range gauge
symmetry in the sense of [54]. Notably, the logic of [54] only rules out global symmetries in
gravity which act in the bulk, and is perfectly compatible with boundary-localized global
symmetries.
Even with this said, the bulk interpretation of our symmetry group G requires an
additional caveat, if we view our input partition function ζ as arising from projecting the
output of some GPI onto an α-sector.
This is because there is no guarantee that the
symmetry group G must be visible as a gauge field in the GPI, or even as a long-range
gauge symmetry of the bulk EFT. One obvious way this can happen is when the symmetry
is associated with a nontrivial bordism class, in which case the associated “gauge flux”
is simply the nontrivial topology of the bulk spacetime itself, as discussed in [59, 196].
For example, in JT gravity, the “gauge flux” associated to ΩSO
1
= Z is merely the bulk
2-manifold itself, viewed as the flux radiated by an EotW brane.
More subtly, the symmetry group G might get washed out in the ensemble over α-
sectors, only reappearing once one projects to a fixed α-sector. This happens in the topo-
logical toy model of Marolf and Maxfield [25], whose ensemble of α-sectors corresponds to
an ensemble of TQMs (as in Section 6.1) with a Poisson-random ground state degeneracy
k. The global symmetry in the α-sector with ground state degeneracy k is U(k), but only
the common diagonal U(1) is visible in the bulk description as the cobordism symmetry
associated to oriented manifolds. The rest of the presumptive bulk U(k) gauge theory is
washed out in the ensemble, with its only remnant being the nonzero mean-field Euler
counterterm visible in the bulk action functional.
One upshot is that we need not get the charged states we may have expected to
come for free when G is non-abelian. If we knew the bulk had dynamical G Yang–Mills
fields, and had made sure to turn on background G gauge fields in our class X of sources,
then we would automatically get charged states from the adjoint action of G on itself.
Perturbatively, these charged states are built from bulk gluons, which transform in the
adjoint representation of G on its Lie algebra, and which provide all neutral states under
the non-invertible 1-form centralizer symmetry of [55, 56]. Globally, this also includes the
states of G bundles on topologically nontrivial manifolds, which correspond to gravitational
solitons and break the non-invertible 1-form centralizer symmetry to the invertible 1-form
center symmetry [57]. When G is not visible in the bulk EFT as a gauge field, or even
as a symmetry of the boundary condition, we will not get these states, which is why all
– 100 –


## Page 103


gravitational states can be neutral under a non-abelian group.
We offer one further interpretation of the symmetry group G, from the perspective on
holography in terms of quantum error correction (see, e.g., [197]). From this perspective,
the collection of semiclassical states in a holographic CFT are described by a code subspace,
in which the semiclassical bulk physics are encoded via a map from semiclassical states to
states of the holographic CFT. From our perspective, the universal construction provides
a canonical code subspace of geometric states in any QFT, though the encoding map will
be far from isometric [198] when the bulk is far from semiclassical.58
Theorem 1.1, in
this language, states that the universal construction is a stabilizer code, meaning, a code
subspace specified as the invariant subspace under a group. More generally, Theorem 1.1
implies that the only families of code subspaces in a local unitary QFT which are compatible
with cutting and gluing of manifolds are stabilizer codes for global symmetries.
11.4
Reflection positivity in the gravitational path integral
A major deficiency of our axiomatic framework, as with any axiomatic framework, is that
it is only as strong as its axioms. We have already discussed the weaknesses of Axiom
4.3, the multiplicativity of ζ, at length, as it corresponds precisely to the original issue of
partition-function factorization. We have also touched on the weaknesses of Axiom 4.1,
the finiteness/well-definition of ζ, which, while severe, are an unavoidable aspect of trying
to reason about the GPI. Axioms 4.2, the reality of ζ, and 4.4, the continuity of ζ are
essentially kinematic, and we do not see any major hurdles arising from either, especially
as the continuity of ζ plays a very minimal role in our construction and results.
It remains to discuss our most important assumption: Axiom 4.5, that ζ is reflection
positive. As in Section 4, we emphasize just how strong of an assumption reflection positiv-
ity really is, particularly when imposed for all possible manifolds and possibly-disconnected
spatial slices. Strong though it is, there are many reflection-positive partition functions,
simply because there are many unitary QFTs. One might wonder, however, whether it
would ever be practically possible to find a reflection-positive partition function without
knowing the underlying QFT in the first place.
Our hope is that the GPI could help us find such reflection-positive partition functions.
This hope applies even in the absence of partition-function factorization; as we have seen in
our review of the proof of the Doplicher–Roberts reconstruction theorem, it can sometimes
be easier to write down an ensemble, prove that it is reflection positive, and project to an
α-sector, than to write down an α-sector quantity directly.
Nevertheless, the output of the GPI is, in general, not reflection-positive. This has
been observed, for instance, in the GPI for three-dimensional pure gravity [103, 140–144],
and was used recently to argue for the axionic weak gravity conjecture [64, 200]. The results
of this paper move the target for the GPI forward: while a generic GPI may not produce a
reflection-positive partition function, our results imply that as soon as one does, then there
exists a unitary holographic dual, which is specified uniquely up to unitary isomorphism.
For instance, consider the ensemble of two-dimensional CFT data studied in [201, 202].
58Even to the point of failing to emerge, in the sense of [199].
– 101 –


## Page 104


Our results imply that the failure of a generic member of this ensemble to satisfy crossing
must correspond directly to actual negativity of the corresponding GPI.59
11.5
Towards fully-extended holography
We close this paper by speculating on the full tower of Factorization Paradoxes described
in the Introduction. The first rung on this tower, as studied by Coleman, Giddings, Stro-
minger, Marolf, and Maxfield [19–21, 25], is the problem of partition-function factorization
arising from Euclidean wormholes, or black instantons. The story does not end there, and
the next rung is Hilbert-space factorization, as studied in this paper. This second rung is
threatened by ER bridges, or black holes.
But Hilbert-space factorization is also not the end of the story.
Just as the baby
universe Hilbert space HBU is only one among the whole family of universal Hilbert spaces
HB, the baby universe category should merely be one among a whole family of universally
constructed categories CK, one for each closed (d −2)-dimensional X-manifold K. The
definition of CK is not quite clear, but the rough picture is as follows. The objects of CK
should be (d−1)-dimensional bordisms B : ∅→K, with categorical wavefunction overlap,
⟨⟨B1|B2⟩⟩= HB2∪KB1,
|Bi⟩⟩∈CK.
(11.2)
The Hilbert space on the right hand side is the universal Hilbert space on the connected
(d −1)-manifold obtained by gluing B2 to B1 along their shared boundary K.
The morphisms in CK should be given, roughly, by bordisms with corners along K.
More precisely, bordisms with corners of opening angle 2π/p along K should form the
non-commutative Lp spaces [203] of morphisms associated to CK. Thus, opening angle π
(p = 2) would give the space of states in HB2∪KB1, and opening angle 2π (p = 1) would give
trace-class morphisms, which can be glued into closed manifolds and fed into ζ. Finally,
and likely only formally, opening angle 0 (p = ∞) would form the morphisms of CK itself,
which would almost certainly be Type III.
We could then ask: do the categories CK factorize on disjoint unions,
CK1⊔K2
?= CK1 ⊗CK2.
(11.3)
This issue, the Factorization Paradox for categories, is threatened not just by black in-
stantons, and not just by black holes, but also by black strings. An obvious obstruction,
related to completeness of the spectrum of charged strings, is the possibility of a global
2-form symmetry in the bulk, related to a global 1-form symmetry of the holographic dual.
So we should build a baby universe 2-category, whose objects are condensates [204] of
closed (d −2)-dimensional X-manifolds K, and prove a higher version of DR reconstruc-
tion, saying that the stack of fiber functors is B2G0 for some abelian group G0, at least
when partition functions and Hilbert spaces already factorize. If only partition functions
factorize, this stack would be BG for some 2-group G, and in complete generality we would
59Working this out in detail would be of independent interest for the purpose of connecting our framework
more concretely to the conjecture of Friedan and Shenker [67] which it, in principle, resolves.
– 102 –


## Page 105


just have some 2-stack of α-sectors. We would learn that any breakdown of factorization
of categories is, again, a red herring, corresponding to an ensemble average of an extended
QFT over this 2-stack.
Yet again, this would not be the end of the story, and we should keep going... until we
landed, finally, on a baby universe d-category, and obtained a higher ensemble average of
fully-local, i.e., fully-extended, functorial, unitary, holographic QFTs. Even the notion of
once-extended functorial QFT is far from clear, outside of the topological case. We hope
that the framework described in this paper might help to find it.
As a final comment, note that the original obstruction to holography arises from Eu-
clidean wormholes, which are simply handles of Morse index one in spacetime. In Euclidean
signature, ER bridges, which obstruct Hilbert-space factorization, are handles of Morse in-
dex two, as in the Euclidean Schwarzschild solution. The full tower of Factorization Para-
doxes, then, arises from the full Morse decomposition of the bulk spacetime, or in other
words, the entire bulk topology. If our framework does generalize to the fully extended
case, we would learn that the bulk topology is, itself, a red herring [205], as envisioned
in the paper [206] immediately preceding ’t Hooft’s introduction [13] of the holographic
principle.

B.2
Continuity in sources
We now establish the continuity of states and operators in the universal construction, as
functions of sources.
Proposition B.4. Fix B ∈BordX
d , and consider the state |M⟩in the universal Hilbert
space HB associated to an X-bordism M : ∅→B. Then |M⟩depends continuously on
sources in the Hilbert space topology.
Proof. Recall that a net |ψi⟩of states in a Hilbert space converges to a state |ψ⟩if and
only if it converges weakly and the norms converge:
|ψi⟩→|ψ⟩⇐⇒⟨ϕ|ψi⟩→⟨ϕ|ψ⟩, ∀|ϕ⟩and ||ψi|| →||ψ||.
(B.16)
The implication (=⇒) is immediate, and the converse (⇐=) follows from,
||ψ −ψi||2 = ||ψ||2 + ||ψi||2 −2Re ⟨ψ|ψi⟩→||ψ||2 + ||ψ||2 −2||ψ||2 = 0.
(B.17)
Now, suppose we have a net Mi ∈BordX
d (∅→B) of source manifolds converging to a fixed
source manifold M ∈BordX
d (∅→B). Both the inner products of |Mi⟩with any fixed state
in Hpre
B
and the squared norms ⟨Mi|Mi⟩are obtained by evaluating ζ on closed manifolds
which depend continuously on Mi (by continuity of the gluing of X-structures). Thus, by
continuity of ζ, the norms of |Mi⟩converge to the norm of |M⟩, and |Mi⟩converges weakly
to |M⟩when tested against a dense subspace, hence everywhere by convergence of the
norm. Thus, the states |Mi⟩converge to |M⟩in the Hilbert space topology, as claimed.

Proof. This follows from continuity of the states |N⟩in sources, as was just shown in

Proposition B.6. The universal Hilbert spaces HB are separable.
Proof. For fixed B, the set of diffeomorphism classes of compact d-manifolds with boundary
B is countable.63 Combining this fact with the assumed separability of the space of sources
on any compact manifold, we may choose a countable dense set {Mi} ⊂BordX
d (∅→B)
of X-bordisms. By Proposition B.4, the Q-linear span of the states |Mi⟩is dense in HB,
and thus HB is a separable Hilbert space, as desired.
B.3
Spacetime symmetries
We now consider the action of spacetime symmetries on the universal Hilbert spaces. First,
we construct a strongly continuous unitary representation of the group IsoX (B) of X-
isomorphisms acting on the Hilbert space HB. This unitary representation is required in
order for the universal construction to be well-defined, as our manifolds are only defined
up to X-isomorphism in any case.

of M by u.
Proposition B.8. The operators bu are unitary, and so extend uniquely to unitary operators
on HB. Moreover, the representation u 7→bu is continuous in the strong operator topology.
Proof. Unitarity of bu follows immediately from the unitarity of u under the †-structure on
BordX
d . To check continuity in the strong operator topology, we use that a unitary group
of operators is strongly continuous if and only if it is weakly continuous at the identity.
For weak continuity, we have

C
Universal property of the baby universe category
In this appendix, we provide a proof of the following universal property of the baby universe
category CBU.
First, we need the notion of a D-valued unitary QFT, for an arbitrary
symmetric monoidal W*-tensor category D.
Definition C.1. Let BordX
d be a unitary bordism category, and let D be any rigidly
generated symmetric monoidal W*-tensor category. A D-valued unitary QFT on BordX
d is
a non-degenerate, unitary, symmetric monoidal functor,

d →D.
(C.1)
Non-degeneracy means that, for every B ∈BordX
d , we have
Z(CB(β)) →idZ(B),
β →0,

in the σ-strong topology.64

BU, we
obtain our desired extension. Finally, when D is Cauchy complete, the extension to CBU
follows from the universal property of W*-Cauchy completion.
